Ákhúnd Mullá Ḥusayn (martyrdom):  One of the sanctified souls who
deserves every kind of praise is Ḥaḍrat-i Ákhúnd Mullá Ḥusayn the older
brother of Raḍíuʼr-Rúḥ. His age reached eighty five but he was very weak.
His throat was very narrow from childhood to the extent that he had
difficulty swallowing one grain of lentil. Always he would eat liquid food
and it would take two hours to finish. Despite such physical limitation, he
endured lots of hardship in the path of the Cause of God. At the end, Ḥájí
Siyyid Mírzá, the Imam who leads Friday prayers, in the public bath of
Manshád twisted the breechcloth, made it wet to become heavier, and started
beating it upon the body of Mullá Ḥusayn such that he almost fainted and
some people had to carry him away from the public bath and delivered him
to his family at his house. At home he went straight to bed and he could not
control members of his body anymore and became paralyzed. He was in this
condition for sometime until he passed away.
All the sacrifices that the people of Yazd have made are because of the
Blessed Beauty and in the second degree because of the Afnáns in Yazd. My
pen and utterance cannot adequately describe what Afnáns have done in
Yazd for the Faith. Although to the letter, they have not taught the Faith to
anyone, but with their morals, deeds and behaviour; this lowly servant is
unable to describe their noble action. The presence of majestic enlightened
group of believers in Yazd is the manifest signs of their behaviour. Their
very existence in Yazd has been the cause of turning many people to the
Faith. High and low have testified that these blessed souls are manifest signs,
uplifted banners,  pure lineage and the source of felicity, chastity and
faithfulness. Although they would not cry out outwardly to their belief,
however all the people of Yazd were certain that they were Bahá’ís and they
were the twigs of the Blessed tree (Extracted and translated from TAY, pp.
132-133)
24.
An-Najaf, just 19 miles from Babylon and in the geographic
neighbourhood of the “cradle of civilization,” was founded in 791AD when
it was chosen as the final burial place of one of the "Twelve Imams," ‘Ali
ibn Abi Talib, the Martyr of Mihrab, Commander of the Faithful. He was
the 4th Caliph of Islam, cousin of the prophet Muḥammad and the 1st Imam
of Shí'ih Islam. Najaf is one of the sacred cities of Iraq, the Door of the
Greatest Prophet's Knowledge, the capital of his jurisprudence,  the son of
the Ka'aba. It is also a source of (marjaiyyah) authority and (taqlid) imitation
acting according to that authority, a cradle of scientific and literary
movements, mother of sciences, piety, poetry, and (jihad) holy war.
THE LOCATION OF THE CITY: Najaf is located on the edge of the western
plateau of Iraq, at southwest of Baghdad the capital city of Iraq, with 160


km far from the capital. It is almost 70 meters above sea level, and is situated
on the longitude of 19 degrees and 44 minutes, as well as on the latitude of
31 degrees and 59 minutes. This city is bordered from north and northwest
by Karbala city (which is 80 km far from the city), and from the east by
Kufah city (which is 10 km far from the city).
FOUNDATION : Indeed, nowadays Najaf is the same Najaf Al-Kufah.
Ancient time there was an Arabian town in which they spread the Christian
monasteries in the city before the Islamic conquer, but the city changed and
became a big city, its range was expanded and its inhabitants became
crowded, this is because of the existence of Imam Ali's holy shrine which
gave the city a mark of holiness and respect. The city then became a center
of religious leadership along with a resort of scientific figures. Najaf is an
Arabic name, which has been derived from (Al-Manjuf), its plural is Najaaf
(means a place where water can not overtop), because it is a highland similar
to a hill which it used to block or prevent water to things close to it. Also
the word Najaf refer to a hill. Najaf was given various names one of which
is   Zahrul Kufah.
THE EXPANSION AND CONSTRUCTION: In the year 170 A.H. after the
appearance of Commander of Faithful Imam Ali's holy shrine, Najaf became
a city with a big number of buildings, as the constructional work of the city
continued gradually until it became a well developed city. Constructional
works of Najaf is divided into 3 stages:
FIRST: Construction stage of the Buwaihi State which extended from year
338 A.H. until 9th century of Hijri, which represents a prosperity and vigor
of Najaf, whereas he built a first fence, surrounded the city, then Abu
Muhammed bin Sahlaan Alwazir Al-Buwaihi built the second fence in year
400 A.H.
SECOND: This stage which occurred between 9th century A.H. until the
middle of 13th century A.H. In this period, the buildings of the city became
old and its brilliance disappeared, due to the war between Turks and
Persians.
THIRD: This is the last stage which began from the middle of the 13th
century A.H. when splendor and promotion returned to the city. Meanwhile,
many constructional, cultural, and service changes occurred after the district
became subsidiary to the Karbala province.
o Between year 550 A.H.- 656 A.H. Abbasid Caliph An-Naasir Li
Deenillah, paid a great attention to the city. The construction work
included repairing of Imam Ali's holy shrine.
o The domestic Company founded a railway in year 1325 A.H. which
connected the city with Kufah.


o In year (1325 A.H. / 1908 A.D.) A Railway was set up by a national
company which connects between Najaf and Kufah.
o In year 1348 A.H. / 1929 A.D. Najaf connected to the Kufah by water
pipes, as pumps were also installed to push water to it. While before
the city was depending on canal, wells and small rivers to take its water
from Furat River which is far from the city.
o In year 1350 A.H. / 1931 A.D. during the period of the successor Sayyid
Ja‘far Hamandi, the regional government opened five doors in the city
fence and planned for a wide square in the southern part of the city,
then the merchants and inhabitants of the city began to build palaces,
houses, coffeehouses, gardens, and shops.
o The city Emirs established various schools, gardens, and parks, and a
wide hospital. This new district was called (Al-Ghaziyah ) which was
derived from the name of the king Ghazi.
o Railway was taken away in the year 1948 A.H. When it became easier
for the cars to carry people between Najaf and Kufah as the road is
made between them.
It is the capital of Najaf Governorate. It is widely considered amongst the
holiest cities of Shia Islam and one of its spiritual capitals, whilst also
remaining the center of Shia political power in Iraq.
Summarized from:  https://www.britannica.com/place/Al-Najaf
25.
Ḥájí Ḥusayn Turk was very brave, steadfast and committed to the
protection of the friends of God. We will quote a few of such instances from
the published literature which clearly demonstrates such qualities:
Ḥájí Ḥusayn Turk’s visit to Bághdád: Translated from the book “Ẓuhúr’ul-
Haqq”, vol. 3, page 194:
“Áqá Mírzá Muḥammad-‘Alí Nahrí along with his nephew and Áqá Mírzá
Muḥammad Ḥasan from Isfahan reached Bághdád and returned after a while
and took along the musk laden breezes from Iraq with them. In this way
people like Áqá Muḥammad Javád Farhádí, Mírzá Muṣṭafá Naráqí, Mullá
Muḥammad Riḍá Raḍíuʼr-Rúḥ, Áqá Siyyid ‘Abdu’l-Báqí Káshí, Sháṭir Riḍá
Ardikání with Ḥájí Ḥusayn Turk and Áqá Ḥusayn Ḥájí Rajab Manshádí one
after another travelled to Bághdád, benefited from the divine knowledge,
and returned to their homeland.”
In “Táríkh Amrí Yazd [TAY]” pp. 170 and 171, the following account has
been recorded which has been translated and shown below:
“Another one of the brave and audacious believers was Ḥáḍrat-i Ḥájí
Muḥammad-Ḥusayn Turk Manshádí who was one the earlier
believers (from the time of the Báb). One day, he was passing


through the street of Manshád, all of a sudden workers of Shaykh
Muḥammad Sabzivárí arrested him and took him away to the house
of the Shaykh. Shaykh told him how is that you, Ḥájí Ḥusayn,
understood the return of Imam Ḥusayn while myself having much
knowledge did not get it? He answered without any hesitation: “look
what happened when Abú Dhar became a believer during the time of
Muḥammad while Abú Jahl with all his knowledge did not?”  Shaykh
became very agitated and angry then ordered to scourge him with
many whip lashes and ordered him to be locked up in a room then he
informed Ibráhim Khan, the governor of Yazd. The government
people came, took him away to the government house; the governor
ordered him to be imprisoned for forty days. Then he asked him to
pay a sum as penalty and after the payment he was released. There
were three brothers: Ḥájí Zaynal, Áqá Ḥasan and Ḥájí Muḥammad-
Ḥusayn. They were known as Turks and were very sanctified souls.
They were not amongst the men of learning but they were the essence
of the love of God. After the martyrdom of the seven martyrs of
Yazd, Ḥájí Muḥammad-Ḥusayn travelled to Ṭihrán and stood in front
of the carriage of Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh and said O king! Have you ever
heard that anyone has given the care and protection of his flock of
sheep to a wolf? And started crying such that he made all those who
were around him very sad and disturbed. The king asked repeatedly
what had happened. The old man who was overwhelmed by crying
was unable to mutter a word. At the end when he gained his
composure said:  “which sins did the people of Yazd commit that the
Point of Adoration of the world (Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh) dominated
Jalálu’d-Dawlih over them?” He asked: “What did he do, O aged
man?” He said: “This wolf tore apart the divine flock of lamb. They
cut off the head of seven men in one day. There was not any act of
tyranny that he did not commit. A learned man of seventy years of
age he murdered with his own hand with bullets from his gun. There
was not any crime that he did not commit. He plundered the property
of the people.” He continued with his explanation while he was
crying.  As a result of his action, Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh deposed Jalálu’d-
Dawlih. Then he returned to Yazd.  He was a true gentleman. The
sons of the Ḥájí Zayanl were: Áqá Abu’l-Qásim who was the
gardener of the garden of Riḍván in ‘Akká until his last breath; and
the other son was Áqá Muḥammad Ibráhím who was serving the
Ancient Beauty in the blessed  House from the time he was a youth
until the end of his life. The family of these three brothers were
engaged in serving the cause in Manshád, the Holy Land, and Egypt:


they were true believers and the servant of humanity. One of them
was late Áqá ‘Alí-Muḥammad, the son of Áqá Ḥasan Turk who was
the Sheriff of Manshád for twenty five years while he was in charge
of the town no body dared to harm the Bahá’ís particularly he
protected the survivors of the martyrs. Members of his family,
specially Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar, his eldest son, protected the friends of God
in Manshád.”
In the book: “Ẓuhúr’u’l-Ḥaq [ZHQ]”, vol. 5, pp. 263-264, the following
account has been recorded which is being translated and shown below:
“Atrocities in Manshád in 1299 AH (1882):    In Manshád through
the mischief of Navváb-i Raḍaví and agitation and incitement of
mullás,  the enemies of the Faith and hoodlums started to harm the
friends. At the beginning they started with the believers who were
meek and low profile in the society and heaped upon them all kinds
of verbal abuse and belittling them. Then they became bolder,  started
plundering their houses and properties, and to set in fire some houses.
Some of these people had to flee to other regions and some went to
Shiraz some to Ṭihrán and others to Rafsanjan, Kirman, Bavánát and
Abádih. Ḥájí Ḥusayn Turk with three others went to Isfahan and
others who could not go away stayed on and accepted all the
hardship. Ḥájí Ḥusayn Turk with his companions tried to plead to the
government and to demand justice. One day they stood on the way
of Ẓilu's-Sulṭán waiting for him to show up and as soon as the prince
drew near to them Ḥájí raised his hand up for pleading for justice,
moaned and groaned.  He complained about the tyranny and
oppression of the ruling class, and complained that the governor is
turning blind eyes to their atrocities thereby allowing them to do
whatever they wanted. Ẓilu's-Sulṭán pulled the bridle of his horse and
stopped and said: “Hey old man, what has happened?” Ḥájí Ḥusayn
Turk said: “I am nearly eighty years of age and am known for years
with the name Bábí.  How would it be possible to change such
reputation? Also this is the case with many other people of Manshád.
Now if we deserve to be killed please order they kill us all at once,
otherwise you should produce an atmosphere of tranquillity and
peace so that we pray for your good health and well being.” The
prince consoled him and told him to go to the government house the
next day so that he would make arrangements for his affairs and those
of others. The next day Ḥájí and companions went to the government
house and complained about Navváb-i Raḍaví. The prince sent a
telegram to Ibrahim Khalil who was the governor on his behalf to this


effect that it would appear that the rule of Yazd is in the palm of
others and if it is not the case why is it that the subjects have dispersed
and fled to the countryside? You have to bring well being and peace
to Manshád and those who have oppressed others should be punished
and then wrote an edict  and gave it to them to take along and submit
it to the governor as follows: “ You have to console the wronged ones
of Manshád and protect them from the mischief of the oppressors and
punish the evil doers.”  Ḥájí Ḥusayn took the edict and returned to
Yazd and gave it to Ibrahim Khalil, the governor. The governor had
no other choice but to punish a few of evil-doers and made it illegal
for anyone to bother them. This was the cause of the further enmity
of tyrants;  Mírzá Imam jum‘ih made a new plot against the faithful.
At midnight he fired some rounds in the air from his pistol and started
shouting so that the neighbours would gather around him and he put
the blame on the Bahá’ís whom he claimed wanted to kill him.”
26.
Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh was the Sháh of Qájár, Iran from 5 September 1848 to
1 May 1896 when he was assassinated. He was the son of Muḥammad Sháh
Qájár and Malik Jahán Khanum and the third longest reigning monarch in
the Iranian history after Shapúr II of the Sassanid dynasty and Tahmasp I of
the Safavid Dynasty.
Born: July 16, 1831, Tabriz, Iran
Buried: May 2, 1896, Shah ‘Abdu’l-‘Aẓím shrine, Ṭihrán, Iran
Assassinated: May 1, 1896, Ṭihrán, Iran
Children: Muẓaffari’d-Dín Sháh Qajar, and others
Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh, (born July 16, 1831, near Tabriz, Iran—died May 1,
1896, Ṭihrán), Shah of Iran (1848–96) who began his reign as a reformer
but became increasingly conservative, failing to understand the accelerating
need for change or for a response to the pressures brought by contact with
the West. Although a younger son of Muḥammad Shah, Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh
was named heir apparent through the influence of his mother. Serious
disturbances broke out when he succeeded to the throne on his father’s death
in 1848, but these were quelled through the efforts of his chief minister,
Mírzá Taqi Khan. Under Taqi Khan’s influence, Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh began
his rule by instituting a series of needed reforms. Taqi Khan, however, was
later forced from power by his enemies, who included Náṣiri’d-Dín’s
mother, and was disgraced, imprisoned, and finally murdered. In 1852 an
attempt was made on Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh’s life by two Bábís; he responded
with a fierce, cruel, and prolonged persecution of the members of this
religion. Unable to regain territory lost to Russia in the early 19th century,
Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh sought compensation by seizing Herat, Afghanistan, in
1856. Great Britain regarded the move as a threat to British India and
declared war on Iran, forcing the return of Herat as well as Iranian


recognition of the kingdom of Afghanistan. Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh was effective
in certain areas. He introduced telegraph and postal services, built roads,
opened the first school offering education along Western lines, and launched
Iran’s first newspaper. He visited Europe in 1873, 1878, and 1889 and was
impressed with the technology he saw there. In the later years of his rule,
however, he steadfastly refused to deal with the growing pressures for
reforms. He also granted a series of concessionary rights to foreigners in
return for large payments that went into his own pockets. In 1872 popular
pressure forced him to withdraw one concession involving permission to
construct such complexes as railways and irrigation works throughout Iran.
In 1890 he made an even greater error in granting a 50-year concession on
the purchase, sale, and processing of all tobacco in the country, which led to
a national boycott of tobacco and the withdrawal of the concession. This last
incident is considered by many authorities to be the origin of modern Iranian
nationalism. Increasingly unpopular among various Iranian factions,
Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh was assassinated in Ṭihrán by a follower of Jamál’ul- Dín
Afghaní.
27.
Jalálu’d-Dawlih: Sulṭán Ḥusayn Mírzá entitled Jalálu’d-Dawlih (1868 –
1913) was a grandchild of Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh and a son of prince Mas‘ud
Mírzá entitled Ẓilu's-Sulṭán  who was in turn the son of Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh
and Hamdam’ul-Mulk (Kawkab’u-Dawlih daughter of Mírzá Taqi khan
Amir Kabir.) Ẓilu's-Sulṭán who was the governor of Isfahan and ruled
almost half of Iran, appointed his son Sulṭán Ḥusayn Mírzá,  Jalálu’d-
Dawlih, to the position of the governor of Yazd (1890 – 1892). In this period
the martyrdom of the seven martyrs of Yazd took place (1891). This was
due to the fatwa given by the clergy and the whole-hearted support by the
governor. Shortly after this, due to complains about his rule and poor
management of the affairs of the city he was removed from the office.
However, he was appointed once again to the governorship of Yazd in 1903-
1905. During his second tenure, the horrendous events of the martyrdom of
nearly one hundred Bahá’ís in Yazd and surrounding villages took place
with utmost cruelty by unruly mobs instigated by the clergy. The governor
was so weak that could not intervene or it was not to his advantage to stop
the clergy. In the Writings he is referred to as the “Tyrant of the land of Ya”
(refer to the Tablet of the World and other Tablets). After he was deposed,
numerous individuals claimed that he owed them large sums of money and
they established it by genuine documents that he could not reject or deny
and also he was not in a position to pay his debt. One of them was a Bahá’í
by the name Mullá Bahrám. During the reign of Muhammad-‘Alí Sháh he
supported the constitutionalists. The king sent him to Europe in exile. He
was in Geneva with his brother and father when they met  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá; he


repented from his past cruel action against the Bahá’ís and tried to put the
blame on others.  Refer to the memoirs of the Juliet Thompson for 28 August
1911. He passed away in 1913.
28.
Martyrdom of Ḥájí Muḥammad Ḥusayn son of Báqir:   Details of events
that lead to the martyrdom of Ḥájí Muḥammad Ḥusayn son of Báqir entitled
Sham‘ush-Shuhadá  have been reported in the following books:
(i)
Yádnámih Bayḍayi Núrá, [YBN] pp. 123-130, 135-136, 146-147
(ii)
Táríkh Shuhadáyi Yazd [TSY], pp. 508-514.
(iii)
Táríkh Amrí Yazd [TAY], pp.  162, 235-244
The following is a summary which is translated from: Táríkh Amrí Yazd
[TAY], p.162. “Haji Muḥammad Ḥusayn son of Báqir:   A brief history
of the life of Ḥaḍrat-i Ḥájí Muḥammad Ḥusayn son of Báqir is as follows
that after he accepted the Faith, he had no rest. Day and night, he  was
constantly arranging meetings for the loved ones of God either in town or in
the farm of Mihdi -Ábád. He was so ablaze with the  fire of the Cause of
God which he could not sit still for a moment. He was busy transcribing
Tablets. His hand-writing was not so good but there are many manuscripts
of the Tablets available with the friends in his hand-writing. This kind of
life continued until he moved to ‘Abbás-Ábád and became busy with the
work of the management of the farm and yet he spent considerable length of
time for the service to the faith and he was there when his martyrdom took
place.”

of several thousand, is more accurate statement.

Mullá Bahrám Akhtar-Khávarí:    The life history of Mullá Bahrám
Akhtar-Khávarí, or in short Mullá Bahrám, is recorded in Maṣábíḥ-i-
Hidáyat, [MAH] vol 4, pages 376-446; it is presented here in very condensed
form.  Mullá Bahrám should be regarded as the foremost Bahá’í teacher
from  Zoroastrian background. ‘Abdu’l-Bahá says in a Tablet something to
the effect that Mullá Bahrám is the mercy of God and a bounty of the Lord;
you should appreciate him greatly (To see the tablet, refer to  [MAH], vol 4,
p. 377). His name was Bahrám, his father’s name Bahman, his grandfather’s
name Jamshíd. He was borne in 1275 AH (1858) in Maryam-Ábád, Yazd;
his mother’s name was Sulṭán khánum Máhyár. As he grew older, he
followed the profession of farming but followed rigorously the religion of
his ancestors which was Zoroastrian. He  was not fanatical about it; he was
meditating deeply on the contents of the text of the Zoroastrian Scriptures.
Soon he realized that the advent of some holy beings had been prophesied:
Húshidar Máh, Húshidar Búmi, Sháh Bahrám Varjávand and Dustúr


Pashútan, so he was always in search of new ideas. Mírzá Isá khán, one of
minister of Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh asked the government of Yazd to send seven
Zoroastrian of Yazd to Ṭihrán for the advancement of a farm; Mullá Bahrám
was one them;   he was considered  an expert in shooting rifle, in
development of domestic animals and in agriculture. He heard about the
name of Faith for the first time in Yazd when one of the believers was
martyred, then  in Ṭihrán he heard the name again. After finishing his work
in Ṭihrán, he travelled to Kashán to work for Kaykhusru Khudádád who was
a Bahá’í. His employer found him fair in his views and genuinely interested
in seeking the truth, so he started teaching him the Faith. This was the first
time that he came in contact with the history and teachings of the Faith. After
two and a half years he returned to Yazd and met Mullá Qalandar then a
bond of friendship developed between them and in the meantime he heard
more about the Faith. Finally, Ḥájí Muḥammad-Ṭáhir Málmírí had serious,
deep and long discussions with him in 1301 AH (1884) which lead to his
acceptance of the Faith. From this point onwards he was on fire and
wherever he went he started the conversation about the Faith. He brought so
many of the fellow Zoroastrian into the Faith. When he read the Lawhi
Mánikchí ( Manekji ) Ṣáḥib, the fire of love of God in his heart inflamed, he
forgot totally about himself. The first opposition came, from inside his
family, from his wife until she passed away a few years later. Mullá Bahrám
was a witness to the events of seven martyrs of Yazd. Dustúr Tir-Andaz  was
one of the learned in the Zoroastrian community of Yazd and was aware of
his association with the Bahá’ís and  tried to harm him. So Mullá Bahrám
travelled to Bombay for one year to allow the enmity to cool off. Upon his
return,  he was chosen as one of the members of the Anjuman-i Náṣiri, a
quasi government organization to look after the interests of Zoroastrians.
For the second time he went to Bombay. When he returned, Afnans made
arrangements such that Jalálu’d-Dawlih to meet Mullá Bahrám. In 1320
(1902) about one year before the great massacre of Bahá’ís to occur,
Jalálu’d-Dawlih asked him to develop the land and property he had in
Píshkúh which had very little water, was in poor condition and he had
bought it at very low price. He told Bahá’ís that because of the name of
‘Abdu’l-Bahá (page 417 MH, Vol. 4)  he called it ‘Abbás-Ábád. With the
encouragement of  Mírzá Maḥmúd Afnan, Mullá Bahrám, with Khusru
Mihrabán and 15 other Zoroastrian families, moved to the farm and made a
contract with Jalálu’d-Dawlih for the development of the farm. Next year
when the time for harvest approached, the upheaval of Yazd started and 84
people (page 418 MH, Vol. 4) were martyred. Jalálu’d-Dawlih  wanted to
collect back all the papers and documents which had been written and the
contract drawn and signed from Mullá Bahrám; but he resisted  and the
government officials and the people of Jalálu’d-Dawlih started  beating  him
up to force him to submit all his legal documents. Since his life was in
danger, Mullá Bahrám left Yazd for Ṭihrán while he had no money or riches


of this world with him. He arrived in Ṭihrán without money, exhausted,
nearly sick and quite weak. So he started to work for Arbáb Jamshid, a
wealthy and influential Zoroastrian in Ṭihrán. In Ṭihrán he was elected as
one of the members of the Spiritual Assembly. Afterwards, he went on
pilgrimage and met ‘Abdu’l-Bahá.  He passed away in 1349 AH (1930)
when he was 79 years old.
31.
‘Abbás-Ábád:   Form [TAY], p. 234:  “‘Abbás-Ábád was the property of
prince Sulṭán Ḥusayn Mírzá entitled Jalálu’d-Dawlih. He asked Áqá Siyyid
Mihdi Afnan and Áqá Siyyid Maḥmúd Afnan to send some of the Bahá’ís
from Zoroastrian background such as Mullá Bahrám and his brother Aqa
Kayumarth and Siyah Bakhsh and several others to send them over there.
Although they were working in Afnan’s farms and they had certain
arrangements with them, nonetheless, Afnans sent them to ‘Abbás-Ábád.
Also he asked if some others for having shops and others in construction,
butchery, and other trades to be sent over there. Therefore, Afnans sent Ḥájí
Siyyid Javad-i Muḥammad-Ábádi and Ḥájí Muḥammad Ḥusayn son of
Báqir and Ḥájí Aḥmad, the shoe-maker and Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar the butcher and
several other Bahá’ís over there to become engaged.” Also refer to [MAH],
vol. 4, p. 417.
32.
Events of 1903 in Yazd:  By 1903, the Bahá’í community of Iran had
experienced almost five decades of relative ease. Not that there was no
persecution at all in this period rather whatever happened was local or
confined to a smaller  region. The last widespread persecution of its
members had occurred in 1852-53.  During the period of half a century, the
Bábí community had changed its character from not hesitating to defend
itself under attack and aggression, to a peace-loving Bahá’í community that
had grown considerably in numerical strength. Yazd was well-known
throughout Iran for its devoted Muslim believers to the extent that they could
be called fanatical (those who disregard any form of rational thinking in
what they believe) and prone to exploitation by the clergy. Therefore there
was always a repressive atmosphere towards the Bahá'ís of the city. At first
matters were moderated by the fact that several of the leading religious
figures in Yazd were indifferent to the new religion while others were
supporters, like Mírzá Muḥammad Riḍá , or even secret believers, like Áqá
Siyyid Abu'l-Qásim Mujtahid. However as the older generation of the divine
learned died, their leadership in Yazd fell into the hands of the mujtahid
Shaykh Muḥammad-Ḥasan Sabzivárí and his son Shaykh Muḥammad-Taqí
who together with Sayyid ‘Alí Mudarris were much opposed to the Bahá'ís.
Perhaps the most interesting development in Yazd during this period was
the conversion of large numbers of Zoroastrians. The first conversion
occurred in Káshán. Several other Zoroastrians had become very much
attracted to the Bahá'í Faith in Ṭihrán during the time that Mírzá Abu'l-
Faḍá’l-i Gulpaygání had been working for Manikji Limji Hatari, the first


Parsi agent in Ṭihrán. But real progress in this area of Zoroastrian
conversions did not occur until Mullá Bahrám Akhtar-Khávarí became a
Bahá'í in about 1298AH(1881). Since he had a thorough knowledge of
Zoroastrian prophecies, he, together with Hájí Qalandar, developed a
successful approach to teaching Zoroastrians of Yazd and some of the
villages around.
There were also conversions in many of the villages around Yazd. In Yazd,
Maryam-Ábád and Mihdí-Ábád, Bahá'í schools were established. In `Alí-
Ábád, Husayn-ábád, ‘Izz-Ábád, Sharaf-Ábád and Hurmuzak strong Bahá'í
communities grew up.
If there was no incitement from the rank of the clergy, the populace lived in
peace with the Bahá’ís. Somehow early in twentieth century the situation
changed.  Áqá Najafí of Isfahan had written letters to all major cities in Iran
encouraging the Mullás to follow his lead in harassing the Bahá’ís. The
newly appointed prayer leader of Yazd, Sayyid Muḥammad Ibráhím, arrived
in Yazd on 12 June, 1903 and was anxious to prove his orthodoxy and to
consolidate his authority. Therefore, before even arriving at the city, he
circulated rumours about a general massacre of the Bahá’ís. The day after
his arrival witnessed the first attacks against several Bahá’í shopkeepers.
The first murder of a Bahá’í took place on 15 June when Ḥájí Mírzá Ḥalabí-
Sáz was brutally killed. After that, for a week, the city was calm but soon
the Bahá’í-killing spread to the nearby villages and cities. During the next
month, wave upon wave of mob attacks left hundreds of Bahá’ís homeless,
nearly 100 of them dead, and many more injured to the extent that in
Manshád alone thirty believers, more than anywhere else lost their lives.
We will insert here, part of a lengthy Tablet of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá which has been
revealed specifically for the events of Yazd and elsewhere for the year 1903.

ﮐﮫ ﻧﺪای اﻟﮭﯽ ﺑﻠﻨﺪ ﺷﺪ و ﺷﺮق و ﻏﺮب ﺑﺤﺮﮐﺖ آﻣﺪ اﻧﻮار ﺷﻤﺲ ﺣﻘﯿﻘﺖ ﭼﻨﺎن ﺳﻄﻮع ﻧﻤﻮد

ﺷﯿﻌﯿﺎن ﺑﮭﺎﺋﯿﺎن ﮔﺮدﻧﺪ ﺣﺘّﯽ اﺳﺮاﺋﯿﻠﯿﺎن ﭼﻨﺎن ﺷﻮر و وﻟﮭﯽ ﯾﺎﻓﺘﻨﺪ ﮐﮫ ﻋﻨﻘﺮﯾﺐ ﻧﻔﺴﯽ از اﯾﺸﺎن
در اﯾﺮان ﺑﺎﻗﯽ ﻧﻤﺎﻧﺪ ﻣﮕﺮ آﻧﮑﮫ ﻣﮭﺘﺪی ﺑﮭﺪاﯾﺖ ﮐﺒﺮی ﮔﺮدد و ھﻤﭽﻨﯿﻦ ﻓﺎرﺳﯿﺎن ﮐﮫ ﻣﻠّﺖ ﻗﺪﯾﻤﮫ

33.
Pool:  The pool was a man-made body of water which served as an open
reservoir and also was used for irrigating the land at lower elevation. The
pool was filled from a smaller stream from an aqueduct.
34.
The book: Yádnámiy-i-Bayḍá’-i-Núrá’ [YBN], edited by Dr. Vahid Ra’fati
which contains partly the history written by Bayḍá’-i- Núrá’.
35.
Township of Taft:  The township of Taft is located in the southwest of Yazd
province. To its west is Fars province. One of the most important mountains
of the province named as Shir Kuh, with an elevation of 4,075 m. is located
here. The weather of Taft is moderate and many kinds of fruit trees are
planted there. The name of Taft is mentioned in many historical and mystical
books of the 9th century AH., along with the name of Shah Nematullah-i-
Vali.
36.
Ḥaḍrat Válá: It means His highness. It is a preface for highly placed people
as a sign of respect.  In this case Jalálu’d-Dawlih.
37.
Qapán wood was a piece of lumber: 7 feet long, and “4x4” inch wide, that
was used to either weigh or carry heavy loads.
38.
This Tablet of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá appears in the book “Yádnámiy-i-
Bayḍá’-i-Núrá’, [YBN] pp. 146-147.

ﺑﻪ ﺧﺪﻣﺖ ﭘﺮداﺧﺘﻨﺪ، از ﻗﺒﻞ ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﺒﻬﺎء ﺑﮕﻮ: ﺑﮑﻮﺷﻴﺪ و ﺑﻪ ~ر ﳏﺒﺖ ﷲ ﺑﺮاﻓﺮوزﻳﺪ و در ان
ﻗﺮﻳﻪ ﺟﻠﻮﻩ ای ﳕﺎﺋﻴﺪ ﮐﻪ ﻋﺒّﺎس آ7د =ﺎءآ7د ﮔﺮدد. و ﻋﻠﻴﮏ اﻟﺘﺤﻴّﺔ و اﻟﺜﻨﺎء ع ع
The provisional translation of this tablet is as follows:
In care of Jináb-i Ḥájí Siyyid Javád,
‘Abbás-Ábád, Jináb-i Ḥájí Ḥusayn,  the glory of God be upon him!
He is God.
O thou who art steadfast in the Covenant! What thou didst write to
Jináb-i Ḥájí was noted. It was full of praises for ‘Abbás-Ábád,
explaining that his Highness (1) is most kind, and the friends of God
have arisen to render services in the utmost degree of truthfulness and
high resolve.  This news induced limitless joy and happiness, because
one of the greatest commands of God and His teachings is that  the
leader must be kind,  and the follower truthful, brave and a well-
wisher. Should these two become combined, they would result in joy,
happiness, everlasting glory and tranquility in both worlds, a joyful
conscience, and attainment unto the longing of heart and soul. Convey
thou, on behalf of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, greetings to the friends who are
engaged in services there and say: “Make ye a mighty effort! Be ye
enkindled with the fire of the love of God, and adorned with grace in
that village so that ‘Abbás-Ábád may become Bahá-Ábád.” Upon thee
be salutation and praise!
(1) Prince Ḥusayn Mírzá Jalálu’d-Dawlih
(Provisional translation by F.S. approved for publication by the BWC)

39. This Tablet has been published in the page 33 of the book: “Aqibat Bikhayr”,
revealed by  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá in honour of Sakínih Sulṭán (Nanih jún). It is also
published in the book: Makátíb-i ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, [MKT] vol. 5, p. 98:

He is God.
O handmaid of the Abhá Beauty! That bright candle among the martyrs obtained
manifest grace from God’s holy Court and was given the honor of attaining unto
His presence in the Abhá kingdom. During his lifetime, he rose up to  the well-
favoured of God, and he was confirmed in attaining unto servitude to His Sanctified
Threshold. Thou art the respected companion of that pure soul. Thou shouldst live
in accordance with his behavior and conduct so that thou mayest become the cause
of his joy and gladness in the eternal realm. The Glory of the Most Glorious be upon
thee.
(Provisional translation by F.S. which has been approved by the BWC for
publication)
40.
“Abá” was a loose outer garment which was long, flowing over  the
body when it was made from wool it was used also for protection from
cold.
41.
See “Táríkh-i Shuhadáy-i Yazd   [TSY]” written by Ḥájí Muḥammad
Ṭáhir Málmírí; and  also “Tarikh Amrí Yazd [TAY]”, pp. 82-85.
42.
Ḥaḍrat Válá: Jalálu’d-Dawlih see Endnote number 36
43.
The fort or the castle of the governor:  The governor of Yazd lived in a
well-guarded castle with a garrison of soldiers to protect him in case of riots
or when the fort would be attacked by hostile forces. It was like medieval
forts in Europe. It could sustain such attacks for a while until reinforcement
come to the aid of the inhabitants.
44.
Nasaqchi Báshi was one of the posts of Safavid rule of Iran which
continued until towards the end of the reign of Náṣiri’d-Dín Sháh; this post
disappeared after his rule. The main duty of Nasaqchi Báshi was to maintain
the order in soldiers’ camp; in the city acted as a guard and the protector of
peace. He was appointed by the Shah.
45.

talks-abdul-baha/529608493/529608493.xhtml?e031a750

46.
A verse from the Qur’án : Surih An-Najm 53: 8-9.


47.
Sadratu’l-Muntahá: It literally means the last tree beyond which no one
could pass.  In the Islamic literature it is a tree planted in heaven.  In the
Bahá’í Writings it refers to Manifestations of God .
48.
An article in the Payám-i Bahá’í:   This article of the Payám-i Bahá’í
has been written by Mah Mihr Gulistanih (Vahdat) in the issue
number 215, October 1997, pages 25-29.
49.
“The Twin Most Sublime Names” (Ismayn Ilyyin): This title was given
by Bahá’u’lláh to two individuals. One was Ḥájí Áqá Muḥammad, the son
of Áqá ‘Abdu’r-Raḥím Qannád Yazdí, and the second one was Siyyid ‘Alí,
the son-in-law of  Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar.  Siyyid ‘Alí was married to Ḥubbíyyih
the grand-daughter of Áqá ‘Abdu’r-Raḥím Qannád Yazdí (See  Ill. 2, the
Family Tree Number 1, in page 19). These two sublime names are
Muḥammad and ‘Alí which have historical significance in the Islamic
world and their combination is also the name of the Báb.
50.
The day of the martyrdom of the Seven Martyrs of Yazd was on Monday,
18th May, 1981. The age of Áqá ‘Alí-Aṣghar on the day of his martyred
was 24 years. (Tárikh Amri Yazd [TAY], p. 81) & Táríkh-i Shuhadáy-i
Yazd [TSY], p. 47.  In the group of  Seven Martyrs of Yazd, there is
another martyr by the same name who was not a brother  of Áqá ‘Ali-
Akbar Kashani;  he was 19 years of age when he was martyred. Refer to
Endnote number 113.
51.
‘Ishq-Ábád (Ashgabat) was a small city in Turkmenistan, at a distance of
less than 50 km from the Iranian border. In the nineteenth century it was
under the Russian rule. It is now the capital of the independent country of
Turkmenistan. Its current population is about one million. Because of the
oppression of the clerics towards the Bahá’ís in Iran many of them
immigrated to‘Ishq-Ábád which soon swelled to a thriving Bahá’í
community of nearly 2000. Such notable individuals such as Mírzá Abu’l-
Faḍl Gulpáyiganí and Nabíl Akbar lived there for some time. The first
Bahá’í temple was built in this city. Later in the twentieth century because
of the take over of the communist regime in Russia, the Bahá’í community
suffered a lot in that land, it was eventually dissolved and ceased to operate.
In the twenty first century the Bahá’í communities in this country has started
to operate once again.
52.
Refer to the Diaries of Juliet Thompson for first of July, 1909 – page 22
53.
Mírzá Abu’l-Faḍá’il Gulpáyigání (1844-1914) was the most famous
Bahá’í scholar of his time. He was the principal of a theological school when
accepted the Bahá’í Faith. He was a fair person amongst the clerics. He was
known for his scholarly debate, the books he has written, his wide-spread


knowledge of the Qur’án, hadith, tafsir, the knowledge of the Old and the
New Testament. The masterpiece of his books is Kitáb-i-Farái’d (Priceless
Jewels). He responded to a cleric from Caucasia who had written refutation
on the Kitáb-i-Íqán. He was imprisoned for being a Bahá’í, particularly in
the year 1300 AH(1883). After that he left Ṭihrán and travelled for sometime
and eventually reached Ishq-Ábád. ‘Abdu’l-Bahá sent him to America in
1901 to diffuse the misinformation given by Ibrahim Khayr’u’llah to the
believers.  Khayr’u’llah wanted to establish himself as the leader of the
Bahá’í community in North America. Mírzá Abu’l-Faḍá’il passed away in
Egypt in 1914.
54.
A tablet revealed in the honour of Fáṭimih Khánum, the daughter of
Sakínih Khánum.  (Refer to Payám-i Bahá’í, issue number 215, October 1997,
pp. 28-29)

ھﻮﷲ


اﻟﮭﯽ اﻟﮭﯽ ان ھﺬاه اﻣﺔ وﻟﺪت ﻓﯽ ﺣﺼﻦ ﻣﺤﺒﺘﮏ و رﺿﻌﺖ
ﻣﻦ ﺛﺪی ﻣﻌﺮﻓﺘﮏ و ﻧﺸﺎت ﻓﯽ ﻣﮭﺪ ﻋﻨﺎﯾﺘﮏ و راھﻔﺖ ﺗﺤﺖ ظﻞ ﻓﺮداﻧﯿﺘﮏ و ﺑﻠﻐﺖ
رﺷﺪھﺎ ﺗﺤﺖ ﻟﻮاء رﺑﺎﻧﯿﺘﮏ. رب اﻧﮭﺎ ذاﻗﺖ ﺻﺮﯾﺮة ﺷﮭﺎدة اﺑﯿﮭﺎ و ﺗﺠﺮﻋﺖ ﻣﻨﺬ ﻧﻌﻮﻣﮫ
اظﺎ ﻓﺮھﺎ ﮐﺎٔس اﻟﺒﻼ ﻓﯽ ﺳﺒﯿﻠﮏ و ذاﻗﺖ ﻋﻠﻘﻢ اﻟﺠﻔﺎ و ﺣﻨﻈﻞ اﻟﻈﻠﻢ و اﻟﻌﺪوان ﻣﻦ
ﺻﻐﺮھﺎ ﻣﻦ اﯾﺎدی ﺗﻄﺎوﻟﺖ ﺑﺎﻟﺠﻮر و اﻟﻈﻠﻢ و اﻟﻌﺪوان و ﻟﻤﺎ ﺑﻠﻐﺖ رﺷﺪھﺎ ﺗﺤﺖ ظﻠﻞ
اﻟﻄﺎﻓﮏ ﻣﻦ ﻗﺒﻞ ان ﺗﺎﺧﺬ ﻧﺼﯿﺒﮭﺎ ﻣﻦ ﺣﻼوة اﻟﺤﯿﺎة و ﺗﺪرک رﻏﺪ اﻟﻌﯿﺶ ﻓﯽ اﻟﻨﺸﺎة
اﻟﺪﻧﯿﺎ ﺧﻠﻌﺖ ﺛﺒﺎت اﻟﺒﻘﺎء ﻣﻦ داراﻟﻔﻨﺎء و رﺟﻌﺖ اﻟﯿﮏ ﻣﺒﺘﮭﻠﺔ اﻟﯽ ﻣﻠﮑﻮﺗﮏ ﻣﺘﻀﺮﻋﺔ
اﻟﯽ ﺟﺒﺮوﺗﮏ ﻣﺸﺘﺎﻗﺔ اﻟﯽ ﻟﻘﺎﯾٔﮏ ﻣﻨﺠﺬﺑﺔ اﻟﯽ ﻣﺤﻔﻞ اﻟﺘﺠﻠﯽ ﻣﺮﮐﺰ اﻻﻧﻮار ﻣﻠﮑﻮت
اﻻﺳﺮار. رب اﻧﮭﺎ ﺳﻠﯿﻠﺔ اﻟﺸﮭﯿﺪ و ﻓﻠﺬة ﮐﺒﺪ ﻣﻦ ﺳﺮع اﻟﯽ ﻣﺸﮭﺪ اﻟﻔﺪا و ﺗﺠﺮع ﮐﺎٔس
اﻟﺸﮭﺎدة اﻟﮑﺒﺮی و ھﻮ ﯾﻨﺎدی ادرﮐﻨﯽ ادرﮐﻨﯽ ﯾﺎ رﺑﯽ اﻟﺮﺣﻤﻦ و اﻗﺒﻞ ﻣﻨﯽ اﻟﻔﺪاء ﻓﯽ
ﺳﺒﯿﻠﮏ ﯾﺎ ﻋﺰﯾﺰ ﯾﺎ ﻋﻼم ﻗﺪ ﻓﺪﯾﺖ روﺣﯽ ﻓﯽ ﻣﺤﺒﺘﮏ و ﻗﺮﺑﺖ ﺛﺎری طﻠﺒﺎً ﻟﻠﻘﺎﯾٔﮏ و
اﺣﺸﻤﯽ اﻟﯽ ﺣﻤﺎک رب اﺟﻌﻞ اﻣﺘﮏ ھﺬه آﯾﺔ رﺣﻤﺘﮏ و راﯾﺔ ﻣﻐﻔﺮﺗﮏ و ﯾﺘﺒﻮع
ﻓﻀﻠﮏ و ﻣﻌﯿﻦ ﺣﯿﺎة اﺑﺪﯾﺔ اﻟﯽ اﺑﺪ آﺑﺎد و ﻣﻤﺮ اﻟﻘﺮون و اﻻﺣﻘﺎب اﻧﮏ اﻧﺖ اﻟﻐﻔﺎر اﻧﮏ
اﻧﺖ اﻟﻐﻔﻮر اﻟﺮؤ ف اﻟﺮﺣﻤﻦ    ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﺒﮭﺎٓء ﻋﺒﺎس

55.
Uda‘t-u Nafsi  It is an Arabic phrase which appears in several prayers in
Arabic for protection. These prayers have been revealed for protection
which are to be recited when anyone is about to leave his house or his town.
This phrase simply means: “I have placed my being in Thy care and trust.”
As an example refer to “Ad‘iyyih-i Ḥaḍrat-i- Mabúb  [AHM]”, p. 197.


56.
Refer to the book “ ‘Áqibat Bikhayr [ABK]”, p.71, a letter in the
handwriting of Mírzá Maḥmúd Zarqání dated 12 Muharram 1345 (23 July
1926)
ﻣﺤﺮم 1345
از ﺣﯿﻔﺎ ﺑﮫ ﯾﺰد ﺟﻨﺎب ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﻮھﺎب ﺷﮭﯿﺪزاده زﯾﺪ ﻋﺰة اﻟﻌﺎﻟﯽ
ھﻮﷲ ﺗﻌﺎﻟﯽ زاده ﺷﮭﯿﺪ ﻣﺠﯿﺪ آه و ﻧﺎﻟﮫ ات ﺑﮫ ﺳﻤﻊ دﻟﺒﺮ ﻋﻨﺎﯾﺖ واﺻﻞ و ﻣﺮام دل و
ﺟﺎﻧﺖ ﻣﯿﺴﺮ و ﺣﺎﺻﻞ ﭼﮫ ﮐﮫ ﺑﮫ ﯾﺎدت ﭘﺮداﺧﺘﻨﺪ و ﻟﺒﺎن ﭼﻮن ﻟﻌﻞ ﺑﮫ دﻋﺎﯾﺖ ﮔﺸﻮدﻧﺪ ﮐﮫ
اﻧﺸﺎءﷲ ﺷﻔﺎی اﻟﮭﯽ ﻋﻨﺎﯾﺖ ﺷﻮد و ﻓﻀﻞ اﯾﺰدی ﺷﺎﻣﻞ ﺣﺎل ﮔﺮدد. ﻣﻄﻤﺌﻦ ﺑﺎﺷﯿﺪ و
ھﻤﻮاره ھﻤﺪم اﯾﺎت رﺣﻤﺎﻧﯽ ﺷﻮﯾﺪ و ﻣﺘﻮﺳﻞ ﻟﻄﻒ و ﻣﮑﺮﻣﺖ رﺑﺎﻧﯽ ﮐﮫ ان ﻓﯿﺾ ﺳﺒﺤﺎﻧﯽ
ﭘﺎﯾﻨﺪه اﺳﺖ و از ﺟﮭﺎن ﺑﺎﻻ داﺋﻤﺎ ﭘﺮﺗﻮ اﻓﺸﺎن و ﺗﺎﺑﻨﺪه و اﯾﻦ ﺑﮫ اﻣﺮ ﻣﺒﺎرک ﻧﮕﺎﺷﺘﮫ ﺷﺪ.
ﻋﺒﺪ ذﻟﯿﻞ زرﻗﺎﻧﯽ   ﺧﻂ اﺻﻞ: ﺣﺒﯿﺐ روﺣﺎﻧﯽ از ﻣﻠﮑﻮت اﻋﻠﯽ ﺗﻮﻓﯿﻘﺎت و اﻣﺪادات
ﻣﻮﻋﻮده ﺣﻀﺮت ﻣﻌﺒﻮد را از ﺑﺮای ان ﺟﻨﺎب راﺟﯽ و اﻣﻞ ﻣﻄﻤﺌﻦ ﺑﺎﺷﯿﺪ و دﻟﺘﻨﮓ و
ﻣﺎﯾﻮس ﻧﮕﺮدﯾﺪ ﺣﺎﻓﻆ و ﺣﺎرس ﺣﻘﯿﻘﯽ ﻣﻌﯿﻦ و ﻧﺎﺻﺮ ﺷﻤﺎ اﺳﺖ. اﻣﯿﺪوارم ﮐﮫ ﺑﮫ آﻧﭽﮫ
ﯽﻗﻮﺷ ﺶﻧﺎﺘﺳا هﺪﻨﺑ .ﺪﯾدﺮﮔ ﻞﺋﺎﻧ و ﻖﻓﻮﻣ ﺖﺳا ﻢﻈﻋا رﻮﮭظ ﻦﯾا ﮫﺘﺴﯾﺎﺷ

ﯾﺰد ﺟﻨﺎب ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﻮھﺎب ﻧﺠﻞ ﺣﻀﺮت ﺷﮭﯿﺪ ﻣﺠﯿﺪ ﺣﺎﺟﯽ ﻣﺤﻤﺪ ﺣﺴﯿﻦ ﯾﺰدی ﻋﻠﯿﮫ ﺑﮭﺎءاﻻﺑﮭﯽ
ﻣﻼﺣﻈﮫ ﻧﻤﺎﯾﻨﺪ  دوﺳﺖ ﺣﻘﯿﻘﯽ ﻋﺮﯾﻀﮫ ﻣﺮﺳﻠﮫ ﺗﻠﻘﺎء ﺣﻀﻮر وارد و ﺑﮫ ﻟﺤﺎظ اﻧﻮر ﻣﺤﺒﻮب ﯾﮑﺘﺎ
ﺣﻀﺮت وﻟﯽ اﻣﺮﷲ روﺣﯽ ﻻﺣﺒﺎﺋﮫ اﻟﻔﺪاء ﻓﺎﺋﺰ. ﻣﻀﻤﻮن ﺑﯿﺎن ﺧﻀﻮع و ﺧﺸﻮع و ﺛﺒﻮت و
رﺳﻮخ ﺑﺮ ﻣﺤﺒﺖ ﺣﯽ ﻗﯿﻮم و ﺗﻤﻨﺎ و ارزوی ﺣﻀﻮر ﺑﻘﻌﮫ ﻧﻮرا ﺑﻮد ﻟﮭﺬا ﻣﻘﺒﻮل و ﻣﻤﺪوح و
ﻣﺸﮑﻮر واﻗﻊ. اﻟﺤﻤﺪ 4
ﻣﻘﺼﻮد و ﻣﺮام دل و ﺟﺎن ﺣﺎﺻﻞ ﮔﺸﺖ. ﺷﻤﺎ را ﺑﺸﺎرت دھﻢ ﮐﮫ اذن
ﺗﺸﺮف از ﺳﺎﺣﺖ اﻗﺪس ﻣﺒﺎرک ﻋﻨﺎﯾﺖ ﮔﺸﺖ. ﭘﺲ در ﮐﻤﺎل وﺟﺪ و ﺳﺮور و ﺷﺎدﻣﺎﻧﯽ و ﺣﺒﻮر
ﻗﺼﺪ ﮐﻮی دوﺳﺖ ﻧﻤﺎﺋﯿﺪ و از ﻓﯿﺾ ﻟﻘﺎء و زﯾﺎرت ﻣﻘﺎﻣﺎت ﻣﺒﺎرﮐﮫ ﻧﻮرا روح و رﯾﺤﺎن ﮐﺎﻣﻞ
ﯾﺎﺑﯿﺪ و ﻧﺼﯿﺐ ﻣﻮﻓﻮر ﺑﺮﯾﺪ. ﺣﺴﺐ اﻻﻣﺮ ﻣﺒﺎرک ﻣﺮﻗﻮم ﮔﺮدﯾﺪ ﻧﻮراﻟﺪﯾﻦ زﯾﻦ رﺟﺐ 1346

ژاﻧﻮﯾﮫ 1928

ﺟﻨﺎب ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﻮھﺎب اﺑﻦ ﺣﺎﺟﯽ ﻣﺤﻤﺪ ﺣﺴﯿﻦ ﺷﮭﯿﺪ ﯾﺰدی ﻋﻠﯿﮫ ﺑﮭﺎءﷲ ﻣﻼﺣﻈﮫ ﻓﺮﻣﺎﯾﻨﺪ.
روﺣﯽ ﻟﻤﺤﺒﺘﮏ اﻟﻔﺪاء ﻋﺮﯾﻀﮫ ﻣﺮﻗﻮﻣﮫ ﺑﮫ ﺳﺎﺣﺖ ﻗﺪس ﺣﻀﺮت وﻟﯽ اﻣﺮﷲ ارواﺣﻨﺎ

ﺧﺮداد از ﺣﯿﻔﺎ ﺟﻨﺎب اﻗﺎی ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﻮھﺎب اﺑﻦ ﺣﺎﺟﯽ ﻣﺤﻤﺪ ﺣﺴﯿﻦ ﺷﮭﯿﺪ ﯾﺰدی زﯾﺪ ﻋﺰه
اﻟﻌﺎﻟﻢ  ھﻮﷲ ﺗﻌﺎﻟﯽ  ﺣﺒﯿﺐ ﻓﻮاد ﻣﺴﺌﻠﺖ ان ﺟﻨﺎب ﺑﮫ ﺳﻤﻊ ﻣﺒﺎرک ﻗﺒﻠﮫ اﺻﺤﺎب رﺳﯿﺪ و
ﻟﺴﺎن اطﮭﺮ وﻟﯽ اﻣﺮ رب اﻻرﺑﺎب  ﻓﺪﯾﺖ ﺧﺪام ﺑﺎﺑﮫ ﺑﺬﮐﺮ ان ﻣﺤﺐ اﻻﺣﺒﺎب ﮔﻮﯾﺎ ﮔﺮدﯾﺪ
ﮐﮫ اﯾﺸﺎن ﻣﺎذون و ﻣﺠﺎزﻧﺪ ﮐﮫ اول ﺑﮭﺎر ﺑﮫ ﮐﻮی ﯾﺎر رﺳﻨﺪ و طﻮاف ﮐﻌﺒﮫ ﺟﻤﺎل ﻣﺨﺘﺎر
ﻧﻤﺎﯾﻨﺪ و از ﻓﯿﺾ دﯾﺪار دل و دﯾﺎر دل دﯾﺪه ای ﻧﻮرا ﯾﺎﺑﻨﺪ ﺗﺎ ﺑﺎ رواﻧﯽ ﭘﺮ روح و رﯾﺤﺎن
ﻣﺮاﺟﻌﺖ ﺑﮫ دﯾﺎر ﻋﺎﺷﻘﺎن ﻧﻤﻮده از ﺷﻤﯿﻢ ﮐﻮی دوﺳﺖ دل ھﺎ را رﺷﮏ روﺿﮫ رﺿﻮان
ﮐﻨﻨﺪ و ﺟﺎن ھﺎ را ھﻤﺪم ﻣﺤﺒﻮب در دو ﺟﮭﺎن. ﻟﮭﺬا ﺑﮫ ﻓﺮﻣﺎﯾﺶ ھﻤﺎن ﻏﺼﻦ دوﺣﮫ رب
ﻣﻘﺘﺪر اﯾﻦ ﻧﻮﯾﺪ ﺟﺎﻧﭙﺮور ﻧﮕﺎرش ﯾﺎﻓﺖ. ﻋﺒﺪ ذﻟﯿﻞ زرﻗﺎﻧﯽ  ﺧﻂ اﺻﻞ: ﺑﺮادر روﺣﺎﻧﯽ
اﻣﯿﺪ وطﯿﺪ ان اﺳﺖ ﮐﮫ ﺑﮫ ﻓﻀﻞ و ﻋﻨﺎﯾﺖ ﻏﯿﺒﯽ ﺑﮫ ﺧﺪﻣﺎت ﺑﺎھﺮه درﻣﺴﺘﻘﺒﻞ اﯾﺎم ﻣﻮﻓﻖ
ﮔﺮدﯾﺪ و ﺑﮫ اﻧﭽﮫ ﻋﻠﺖ و ارﺗﻘﺎء و اﺷﺘﮭﺎر اﺋﯿﻦ ﮔﺮاﻧﺒﮭﺎﺳﺖ ﻣﻔﺘﺨﺮ و ﻣﻮﯾﺪ ﺷﻮﯾﺪ ﻗﺪم را
ﺛﺎﺑﺖ ﻧﻤﺎﺋﯿﺪ و در ﮐﻤﺎل اطﻤﯿﻨﺎن در ﺗﺮوﯾﺞ ﮐﻠﻤﺔ ﷲ ﺳﻌﯽ ﺑﻠﯿﻎ ﻣﺒﺬول دارﯾﺪ ﺑﻨﺪه اﺳﺘﺎﻧﺶ

ﯾﺰد ﺟﻨﺎب آﻣﯿﺮزا ﻋﺒﺪاﻟﻮھﺎب ذﺑﯿﺤﯽ ﻣﻼﺣﻈﮫ ﻓﺮﻣﺎﯾﻨﺪ ﯾﺎر روﺣﺎﻧﯽ ﺗﺤﺮﯾﺮ ﻣﻨﯿﺮ در

pilgrimage:


ﺣﺒﯿﺐ روﺣﺎﻧﯽ، اﻣﯿﺪوارم اﺳﺒﺎب ﻣﺴﺎﻓﺮت ﺑﮫ ارض اﻗﺪس ﺑﻄﻮر دﻟﺨﻮاه ﻓﺮاھﻢ ﮔﺮدد
و ﺑﮫ آﻧﭽﮫ اﻣﺎل و آرزوی دل و ﺟﺎن اﺳﺖ ﻧﺎﺋﻞ ﮔﺮدی ﺑﻨﺪه اﺳﺘﺎﻧﺶ ﺷﻮﻗﯽ

62.
Munír Nabíl-Zádih was a dedicated teacher of the Cause of God. His life
history is detailed in Maṣábíḥ-i-Hidáyat [MAH], Vol. 4, pp. 220-300. We
will present a brief summary of his life history. Mírzá Munír Nabíl- Zadih’s
grand father was Shaykh Muḥammad surnamed Nabíl by the Báb. He was a
respected merchant in Qazvin who accepted the message of the Báb and was
the first one to do so in his family. Mr. Nabil-Zadih’s father was Shaykh
Muḥammad-‘Alí who believed in Bahá’u’lláh when there were great
proponents of Mírzá Yaḥyá in Qazvin. Nabíl-Zádih was also a nephew of
Shaykh Kázim Samandar, one of the Apostles of Bahá’u’lláh.  Mírzá Munír
Nabíl- Zádih was born in Qazvin in 1297 AH (1880). He was engaged in
trade as a merchant in Rasht.  He travelled to Baku for teaching the Cause
of God in 1318 AH (1901) and then to Tbilisi (Capital of Georgia) and from
there to Ashgabat and travelled to a number of cities in Turkmenistan and
neighbouring countries. When ‘Abdu’l-Bahá was in Egypt called him to His
presence and then he started teaching the Cause in Afghanistan and in
Khurásán. At the beginning of the Formative Age, he was teaching in Burma
and he reported that the number of Bahá’ís in Rangoon was 740 at that time.
Finally he passed away in Ṭihrán when he was 78 years old in 1328 Shamsi
(1949). He has written a number of books one of which is Tibiyyan-i
Haqiqat.
63.
Ḥájí Muḥammad-Ṭáhir Málmírí known as Málmírí, a native of Yazd
from the neighbourhood of Málmír. He was born about the year 1269
AH(1852). We will make a brief summary from the story of his life in the
Kháṭirát-i Málmírí and Masábiḥ-i Hidáyat, vol 5, pp. 300-390.  The name of
his father was Ḥájí ‘Abdu’l-Ghafúr and the name of his mother was
Ma‘sumih. Ḥájí ‘Abdu’l-Ghafúr heard about the claims of the Báb from
Siyyid Ja‘far who was his brother-in-law and likewise accepted the message
of the Báb. In this way, the new Faith  spread among the members of the


family and Muḥammad-Ṭáhir accepted the message of the Báb and
Bahá’u’lláh as he grew up.  He attended the kind of schools for young
children which was available at the time. From tender age he started learning
the craft of weaving and cloth making. He was eager to learn and spent his
time studying the Writings of the new Faith and verses from the Qur’an, and
verses from Torah in Hebrew. He started memorizing passages regarding
prophesies for the new Faith from the Qur’an, Hadith, Zoroastrian books,
and the Bible. Soon he became a capable teacher of the Cause. He was
instrumental in helping many a soul to understand the Truth such as Ḥájí
Muḥammad Afshár,  Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl  Gunduli, Ḥájí Mírzá Aḥmad
Kázim Uf known as Shirazi and  Ḥájí Ja‘far Uyzdárání. In his conversations
in firesides he related stories from the lives of prophets as was directed by
Bahá’u’lláh during his pilgrimage. He was well known in Yazd and
everyone knew that he was a Bahá’í. When he was walking in streets nobody
dared do or say something disrespectful or denounce the Central Figures of
the Faith. He survived the massacre of Yazd in 1903. He was arrested as a
member of the Spiritual Assembly of Yazd on false charges and imprisoned
and defended himself in court in Ṭihrán. He has written a number of books:
Kháṭirát-i Málmírí, Tárikh Amri Yazd and Tarikh Shuhadáyi Yazd. He
received nearly fifty Tablets from Bahá’u’lláh and  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. For
nearly 80 years he was teaching the Cause of God. He lived nearly one
hundred years and passed away on 14 Khurdád 1332 Shamsi (4 June year
1953),  110 Badi‘ , 1372 AH.
64.
Validity of Pilgrims’ Notes:  Individuals who have gone on pilgrimage,
particularly during the time of Bahá’u’lláh, ‘Abdu’l-Bahá and Shoghi
Effendi, have admitted that their experience has been overwhelming and
unusual. These souls when happened to be in the presence of the
Manifestation of God, the Centre of the Covenant or Shoghi Effendi had a
feeling that they were in a different world. So most of their observations or
what they heard was an interaction between their personal feelings at the
time and the reality of the situation. Some of them tried to leave for posterity
what they heard during such occasions. We realize that whatever they
recorded, were their recollections as a whole after they were dismissed from
the Holy presence. At times, it may have taken some time before they could
find a chance to write what they remembered. Therefore, what they recorded
were not necessarily the exact words uttered by these Holy Beings; and apart
from that, it was according to their own understanding of what was said.
Although these notes might contain interesting materials but remain as
personal information. It is obvious that such materials which are
characterized as “pilgrim’s notes” do not constitute a part of the


authoritative text. Also such pilgrim’s notes  cannot be in conflict with the
body of the authoritative text of the Bahá’í Faith.
65.
Ḥájí ‘Abdu’r-Raḥím Qannad Yazdi was the father of Áqá Muḥammad
Yazdí and grandfather of  ‘Azíz Yazdí. Refer to Ill. 2, the Family Tree
Number 1, page 20.
66.
Keith Ransom-Kehler was born Nannie Keith Bean on 14 February 1876.
The family lived in the Cincinnati area. Her early education  was at the
Bartholomew in English and Classical School for Girls in Cincinnati and
later attended Miss Grant’s School in Chicago. She received a bachelor’s
degree in child psychology in 1898. In 1901, at the age of twenty-five, she
married Ralph Ransom, a young painter. She attended Albion College in
Michigan in 1903–04, earning a Master’s degree in 1904, and then taught
English literature there in 1904–05; In 1908, Ralph Ransom died, apparently
of tuberculosis. In October 1910 Keith Ransom married James Howard
Kehler, a successful and innovative advertising executive with an office on
Michigan Avenue in Chicago. Her husband died unexpectedly of a heart
attack on 19 June 1923; Ransom-Kehler became a Bahá’í in May 1921.
Shortly after becoming a Bahá’í, Keith Ransom-Kehler gained recognition
as a Bahá’í speaker, writer, and administrator. In 1924–25 she served as
secretary of the Spiritual Assembly of the Bahá’ís of Chicago. Early in 1926
Keith Ransom-Kehler went on pilgrimage to the Bahá’í holy places in
Mandatory Palestine . On returning from her pilgrimage in the spring of
1926, Keith Ransom-Kehler intensified the pace of her activities.  In 1920’s
and 30’s she widely travelled to the West Indies, concentrating on Barbados.
Her itinerary included Hawaii, Japan, China, Australia, New Zealand, Java,
Singapore, Burma, and India. She travelled to Iran in 1932. She was to
submit a petition to Reza Shah Pahlavi (reigned 1925–41) to remove the ban
on the entry and distribution of the Bahá’í literature in Iran and also to secure
the lifting of all the limitations that had for years been imposed on the
Iranian Bahá’í community. Keith Ransom-Kehler travelled widely
throughout Iran for more than a year.
She had endured periods of extreme hardship. Thus, exhausted and
weakened, she left Ṭihrán, planning to visit several cities in the southern part
of Iran before leaving the country. In Isfahan she maintained her usual busy
schedule until she suddenly fell ill on 9 October 1933 after a full day of
activities. Exposed to smallpox, possibly by cuddling a child recovering
from the disease, she had no physical resistance. She died two weeks later,
on 23 October. Shoghi Effendi called her the "FIRST AND DISTINGUISHED
MARTYR" 1 of the American Bahá’í martyr and appointed her a Hand of the
Cause of God, "INTERNATIONAL SERVICES ENTITLE HER EMINENT RANK


AMONG HANDS OF CAUSE OF BAHÁ’U’LLÁH."2 In 1940 Shoghi Effendi
named her, along with May Maxwell and the indefatigable international
teacher Martha Root, as one of "three heroines of the Formative Age of the
Faith of Bahá’u’lláh"3 to whom the Bahá’ís owe a debt of gratitude that
"future generations will not fail to adequately recognize.”

Notes
1. Cablegram of Shoghi Effendi, October 30, 1933; see page 3, Messages to
America, 1932 to 1946.
2. Cablegram of Shoghi Effendi, October 30, 1933; see page 3, Messages to
America, 1932 to 1946.
3. Letter of Shoghi Effendi, April 15, 1940; see page 40, Messages to America,
1932 to 1946.
4. Also see Bahá’í World, vol. 5, pp. 389-411.

67.
Ḥájí Maḥmúd Qassábchí, a dedicated Iraqi Bahá’í whose accepting the
Faith in 1911 has a very interesting story behind it. He, during the time of
‘Abdu’l-Bahá, assisted with renovation and reconstruction of the Most
Great House which unfortunately did not take long to fall into the hands of
the enemies of the Cause.  Shortly after Shoghi Effendi assumed his
stewardship as the Guardian of the Bahá’í Faith, Ḥájí Maḥmúd Qassábchí
assisted with the construction of additional three rooms along the south side
of the Báb's Sepulchre.  Thus the shape of the building was changed from
an oblong edifice to one perfectly square, which would better lend itself, in
time, to embellishment by addition of an outer structure embodying the
graceful, yet majestic and lofty features desired by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá to honour
the resting-place of the Martyr Prophet who heralded the advent of
Bahá’u’lláh.  Shoghi Effendi named a door on the eastern side for Ḥájí
Maḥmúd Qassábchí of Iraq. In 1933 Qassábchí suffered a severe attack of
paralysis which he narrowly survived and as a result of which he could
hardly move or speak for the rest of his life. On September 11, 1947
Qassábchí, who had already grown frail and weak, fell ill, and two days later
winged his flight to the Abhá Kingdom. On the morning of September 13,
1947 this faithful, steadfast and devoted Bahá’í who loved and served the
Faith of Bahá’u’lláh until his last breath, passed away. He was buried at
Salman Pak, about thirty miles southeast of Baghdad.
68.
Kavákib’ud-Duríyyih (meaning luminous stars); It is a Bahá’í history
book in two volumes written by Ávárih who was a travel teacher of the
Cause of God during the lifetime of  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. But during the time of


the Guardian, he disobeyed him and placed himself and his ambitions above
the decisions of the Chosen Branch, Shoghi Effendi. He did not want to
heed, or correct his mistakes; and continued to create disunity in any
community he entered. Eventually he was sanctioned and ex-communicated
from the Faith and was counted amongst the Covenant Breakers. His two
volumes of history authored by him was written before becoming a
Covenant Breaker. The Master had seen the book and had made some
corrections on the margins.
69.
Tablet of Ram is one of the Tablets of Bahá’u’lláh in which He prohibits
explicitly and unequivocally drinking of alcohol and alcoholic drinks.
70.
The Ḥadith is the collected traditions of the Prophet Muhammad, Shi‘ih
imams and even Prophet’s companions based on their sayings and actions.
There are six canonical ḥadith collections that are widely accepted by Sunni
Muslims and much more for Shi‘ih Muslims; the two most famous are those
of Muhammad ibn Isma‘il al-Bukhárí (810–870) and Muslim ibn al-Hajjaj
(817–875), both of which have the same title aṣ-Ṣaḥíḥ (The Authentic). Each
ḥadith usually begins with the chain of the narrators (isnád) going back to
the time of the Prophet Muhammad, his companions, or Shi‘ih imams which
is then followed by the text of the tradition itself. Al-Bukhárí compiled his
compendium of hadiths with the utmost caution with regards to the
authenticity of the hadith being transmitted. The hadiths relate to a broad
range of subjects, from theological concerns such as revelation and the
nature of God, to instructions on daily worship and rituals, to legal advice.
71.
Uṣúl al-Káfí (Arabic:أﺻﻮل اﻟﻜﺎﻓﻲ
)is the title of the first section the Kitabi al-
Káfí which has three parts. It has universal acceptance among  Shi‘ih and
all of the hadith mentioned there are regarded significant. In this part of al-
Káfí, we can find hadiths concerning the beliefs of Shi‘ih, the biographies
of Imams, and how a Muslim should behave. Uṣúl al-Káfí is the most
significant source of hadiths for the Shi‘ih beliefs. It has frequently been
published as a separate book, it has been translated by several people, and
numerous commentaries have been written on it.
The author of Uṣúl al-Káfí is Abú Ja'far Muḥammad ibn Ya'qúb  ibn Isḥaq al-
Kulayni al-Razi (329 AH and 940AD) known as Thiqat’ul-Islam al-Kulayni.
He was a scholar of ḥadith in the period of Minor Occultation. He was among
the greatest Shi‘ih ḥadith scholars and the author of al-Káfí, the most authentic
Shi'ih ḥadith collection. He was born after the martyrdom of Imam al-'Askari
at the time of Imam al-Mahdi and met some hadith transmitters who directly
had heard hadiths from Imam al-Hadi and Imam al-‘Askari. It is said that al-
Kulayni was very accurate in transmitting ḥadiths.


72.
Biḥár al-Anvár “ﺑِﺤﺎرُاﻷَﻧﻮار
,”is the initial word from a longer phrase which
literally means: Seas of lights: the collection of pearls of the reports of the
pure Imams. It is generally known by its shorter name as Biḥár al-Anvár. It
is the most comprehensive Shí'ih ḥadith collection written under supervision
of al-‘Allama al-Majlisi. Writing of this book which is a collection of Shí'ih
teachings, lasted more than 30 years and a group of al-‘Allama al-Majlisi's
students helped him in this task. The author has organized the book based
on 25 general topics and thus in 25 volumes. However, nowadays it is
published in 110 volumes. Al-Majlisi has tried to cover all common topics
and issues in Biḥár al-Anvár.  Due to its maximum number of ḥadiths
narrated from Shí'ih Imams, chaptering of topics, explanation of ḥadiths,
various theological, historical, jurisprudential, exegetical, ethical, ḥadith,
and lexical researches, Biḥár al-Anvár has a great position among
researchers; so that regardless of its many volumes, since its first
publication, many manuscript copies of that were written and with later
development of printing industry, all or parts of it have been frequently
printed.
73.
Ḥusayn is a grandson of the Prophet Muḥammad. His father is ‘Ali ibn Abu
Talib (cousin of the Prophet Muḥammad and His son-in-law); his mother is
Fatimah, the daughter of Prophet Muḥammad and his older brother is Ḥasan.
74.
‘Alí is the son of Abu Talib (uncle of the Prophet Muḥammad). Imam ‘Alí
is the cousin, son-in-law, and the successor of Prophet Muḥammad
according to Shi‘ih. He is called the Commander of the Faithful. Fatimah,
the daughter of Prophet Muḥammad was his wife. Ḥasan and Ḥusayn were
his sons from Fatimah.
75.
Fáṭimih was the daughter of Prophet Muḥammad and the wife of imam ‘Alí
ibn Abu Talib.
76.
Salmán al-Fársí (Arabic: ﺳﻠﻤﺎن اﻟﻔﺎرﺳﻲ
)was a Zoroastrian and Persian who
became one of the companions of the Prophet Muḥammad and of Imam ‘Alí,
and was very much trusted by Him. The Prophet liked him and said about
him, "Salman is one of us". His idea of digging a trench in the Battle of
Khandaq brought victory to Muslims. He supported the successorship of
Imam ‘Alí after the demise of the Prophet and opposed the incident of
Saqífa. He was assigned as the governor of al-Mada’in in the time of the
caliphate of Umar son of al-Khattab. He gave his salary to charity and
knitted baskets for a living. After a long life, Salman passed away in 36 AH
(656AD) in al-Madyan, where he is buried in a shrine known as "Salman-i
Pák".


Based on some reports, Salmán was a Zoroastrian Iranian whose original
name was Ruzbih. He converted to Christianity in his youth. After hearing
the Christians foretelling the emergence of a prophet in the land of Arabs,
he set off toward Hijaz. He was enslaved in the middle of the way and sold
to a man from Banu Qurayza in Medina. He entered Medina when prophet
Muhammad had recently emigrated to the city. Salman met the Prophet and
after confirming the signs of prophethood converted to Islam. The Prophet
bought and freed him and named him "Salmán".
77.
Abú Dhar : His name Jundab son of  Junáda son of Sufyán al-Ghifárí
(Arabic: ﺟُﻨﺪَب ﺑﻦ ﺟُﻨﺎدة ﺑﻦ ﺳﻔﯿﺎن اﻟﻐِﻔﺎري
)33 AH (653 AD) known as Abú Dhar
al-Ghifárí (أﺑﻮذر اﻟﻐﻔﺎري
)was one of the greatest companions of Prophet
Muḥammad and a companion of Imam ‘Ali ; he was also one of those who
were known as the four pillars of the Shi‘ih. He was a true companion of
Prophet Muḥammad. Numerous virtues and excellences have been narrated
for him by both Sunni and Shi‘ih. Abú Dhar criticized the actions of
Uthman, the third Caliph. As a result, he was exiled to Syria and then to al-
Rabadha, where he passed away.
Abú Dhar was among the first people who converted to Islam. According to
some narrations, he was a monotheist before the emergence of Islam.
According to a narration, Abú Dhar was the fourth one to believe in the
Prophet.  Also Imam ‘Ali has said about him that  ordinary people are unable
to achieve the knowledge of Abú Dhar. Imam ‘Ali also considers him among
those people that Paradise awaits them. Abú Dhar passed away in 32 AH
(653 AD), in al-Rabadha, in the time of Uthman's caliphate.
78.
Khurásán is an eastern province of Iran bordering Turkmenistan to the
north and Afghanistan to the east. Khurásán, historical region and realm
comprising a vast territory now lying in north-eastern Iran, southern
Turkmenistan, and northern Afghanistan. The historical region (which was
much larger than the current province) extended, along the north, from the
Ámú Daryá (Oxus River) westward to the Caspian Sea and, along the south,
from the fringes of the central Iranian deserts eastward to the mountains of
central Afghanistan; it was like a country by itself. Arab geographers even
spoke of its extending to the boundaries of India. The history of the area
stretches back to very ancient times. It was part of the Achaemenian Empire
of the 6th to 4th century BCE and the Parthian empire, which spanned from
the 3rd century BCE to the 3rd century CE. After the Arab conquest in 651–
652 CE, the name was retained both as the designation of a definite province
and in a looser sense. It was occupied by the Afghans from 1722 to 1730.
Nádir Sháh, born in Khurásán, broke the Afghan supremacy and made


Mashhad the capital of his Iranian empire. Firduwsí, author of the Sháh-
námih (“Book of Kings”), and Umar Khayyám, the celebrated poet and sage,
were born in the region. Khurásán’s current Iranian frontiers were defined
in 1881 and in a convention of July 8, 1893. Khurásán, as a result of its
troubled history, is peopled by a great variety of ethnic groups: Turkmen in
the northwest; Kurds around Bujnúrd and Qúchán; Tímúrís and Jamshídís
in the east, some of whom are still nomadic; farther southwest, Ḥeydarīs;
and southeast, Baluch. The highlands in the south are home to a settled
population of Iranian ethnicity. Here and there are found Berberis of Mongol
origin, Arabs, Roma, and a few Jews in the towns.
Modern Iranian Khurásán is largely agricultural, producing fruits, cereals,
cotton, tobacco, oil plants, saffron, and some silk. Livestock are plentiful;
wool, lambskins, and goat hair are exported, and poultry is also raised. The
mineral products include turquoise, salt, iron, copper, lead, zinc, chromium,
magnesite, and coal. Cement, processed foods, ginned cotton, carded wool,
sugar, pharmaceuticals, animal fodder, and textiles are the manufactured
products. Handicrafts include jewellery, rugs and carpets, furs, dolls,
glassware, and handloomed cloth. A railway and roads link Mashhad, a
thriving city, with Iran’s capital, Ṭihrán.  (Extracted and summarized from
Encyclopaedia Britannica)

81.
Shaykhís are followers of Shaykh Aḥmad al-Aḥsá’í (1753–1826), a
Twelver Shiʿih Islamic thinker who emphasized the esoteric and intuitional
aspects of the religion.  Shaykh Aḥmad cultivated followers in al-Aḥsa in
eastern Arabia, Baḥrain, and Iraq, as well as in Yazd and Kermanshah in
Iran. A separate school of Shiʿism did not coalesce around his name until
after his death, when he was succeeded by Siyyid Kázim Rashtí (d. 1844) in
Karbala; for a time, esoteric Shaykhísm offered a challenge to the scholastic
orthodoxy of the Uṣúlí school.
After Rashtí's death, though many Shaykhís became Bábis, important
Shaykhi communities continued to exist in the Persian Gulf, and in Kerman
and Tabriz. The Tabriz Shaykhís diminished their doctrinal and ritual
differences with the majority Uṣúlí school, and played a progressive role
during Iran's Constitutional Revolution (1905–1911). The Kerman
Shaykhís, led by the Qajar noble Karím Khan Kermaní (1810–1871) and his
descendants, remained esoteric. Kerman Shaykhism proved conservative
and often was supported by local governors.


In the twentieth century Shaykhísm became marginalized as a Shiʿih sect
and Shaykhis suffered persecution under the Islamic Republic of Iran after
1979. Shaykhí communities persist in Kermán and elsewhere. The Iḥqáqí
family (based in Kuwait but originally from Tabriz) now produces most
Shaykhi publications and claims many followers in southern Iraq. The
movement also has been prominent in recent decades in Pakistan, where
debates have raged over its orthodoxy and a Shaykhí Institute was founded
in Faisalabad.
82.
Bam, city in eastern Kermán province, Iran. The city, an agricultural center
situated on the Silk Road and long famed for its large fortress, was
designated a UNESCO World Heritage site in 2004. Bam is located about
115 miles (185 km) southeast of the city of Kermán at an elevation of
approximately 3,600 feet (1,100 meters) on the banks of the Bam River. The
region around the city has long been known for its date palms, which are
among the most productive in Iran, although cotton and various grains are
also raised there.
The modern city is located immediately to the south of the site of the ancient
citadel (arg) Arg-i Bam, once one of the world’s largest mud-brick
complexes. Located on a hilltop, the citadel consisted of a series of three
concentric walls made of mud brick and palm timbers, the outer wall of
which enclosed the old city. Bam’s highest point, the citadel proper, rose to
about 200 feet (60 meters) above its base. The walls of the fortress, some 40
feet (12 meters) high, were once surrounded by a deep moat. The citadel
was established during the Sasanian dynasty (224–651 CE); even as late as
the beginning of the 19th century, it was the strongest fortified place in Iran,
having been used most extensively during the Iranian dynastic disputes of
the 18th and 19th centuries. During that same period, the fortress fell into
the hands of invading Afghan armies, and much of the population fled.
By the late 19th century the establishment of secure borders and a strong
centralized state made such a military stronghold obsolete. The old city site
was abandoned by the end of the century, though the fortress continued to
be used as a military garrison until the 1930s. Beginning in the 1950s, the
Iranian government began to restore the fortress, but in 2003 the region
around Bam was struck by a massive earthquake that killed more than
26,000 people and devastated the modern city, and the fortress itself was
largely destroyed. Pop. (2006) 75,215.
83.
Abarkuh and the local populace call it Abarqu. It is a small town on the
border between the Iranian provinces of Fars and Yazd. The town is placed
as being in Fars on many maps while many writers place it within Yazd


province, the main township of Abarkuh district. It sits at an altitude of 1509
metres (4954 feet) and is some 140 kilometres (87 miles) to the west of the
city of Yazd, and about 300 km north-east of Shiraz, on the main road that
connects Yazd to Shiraz via Persepolis.
Abarku is home to a 4,500 year old cypress (Persian, sarv) tree that stands
some 25 metres tall. Its trunk has a diameter of about 4.5 metres and a
circumference of about 11.5 metres. Many Zoroastrians revere this ancient
tree as sacred. Some tie ribbons on its branches and make a wish. When the
wish is fulfilled, they return to untie the ribbon. The age of the tree is at least
4,000 years. For Zoroastrians, the cypress (Persian, sarv) is rich with
symbolism. It is a symbol of life - a tree of life. Abarkuh is located in the
desert region with hot and dry weather. This historic city once saw
prosperous days as it was located on the ancient trade road, the Silk Road.
The incidence of Abarqu which led to the arrest of the members of the
Spiritual Assembly has been described by Mr. ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb and we will
not go into further details.
84.
Bandar-i ʿAbbás, port city and capital of Hormozgán province, on the Strait
of Hormuz, the main maritime outlet for much of southern Iran. It lies on
the northern shore of Hormuz Bay opposite the islands of Qeshm, Lárak,
and Hormuz. The inhabitants are mainly Arabs and African. The summer
climate is oppressively hot and humid, and many inhabitants then move to
cooler places; however, winter is pleasant.
Bandar-i ʿAbbás (“Port of ʿAbbás”) was established in 1623 by Sháh ʿAbbás
I to replace the city of Hormuz, which had been captured by the Portuguese
about 1514. During the 17th century it was the main port of Persia, but it
lost this status in the 18th century to the rival “Port of Búshihr” (Bandar-i
Búshihr). From about 1793 Bandar-i ʿAbbás was under lease to the rulers of
Muscat, but in 1868 Iran canceled the contract and resumed direct control.
The port’s imports consist mainly of manufactured goods. Its exports
include Kermán rugs, petroleum products, and agricultural produce. The city
has a cotton mill, a fish cannery, and refineries for oil and gas. The roadstead
is shallow and badly sheltered, and vessels must sometimes lie 4 miles (6.5
km) out. Despite the poor quality of its port facilities, the city boomed during
the Iran-Iraq War of the 1980s when Iran’s more westerly ports were
threatened. A new harbour and shipbuilding yard were under construction
in the late 20th century west of the existing port, and a major rail link was
completed in 1995. Pop. (2006) 379,301. (Extracted from Encyclopædia
Britannica, Inc.)


85.
Rafsanjan: With a population of 400,000, Rafsanjan is located in the
northwest of Kerman Province. About 45% of its population live in rural
areas. The distance between Rafsanjan and the city of Kerman is 110
Kilometres. The city encompasses a land area of about 7678 square
kilometres.  Rafsanjan is surrounded by Zarand from the north, Shahr-i-
Babak from the west, Bardseer from the south, and Kerman from the east.
Rafsanjan is known for its good quality pistachio. This product constitutes
one of Iran's most principal exports, and Rafsanjan is the epicentre of
pistachio production and the largest exporter of this product worldwide. The
desert region of Rafsanjan has relatively hot summers and chilling winters.
There is an expanse of mountainous areas around the city that largely affect
its climate. Rafsanjan’s pistachio orchards are of high repute globally.
Pistachio cultivation and trading is people's number one engagement. The
city is also home to some of Iran's largest tiles and ceramics manufacturers.
Rafsanjan is the hub of carpet production even though the rugs are offered
for sale as Kermani rugs rather than Rafsanjani ones. Another remarkable
employer is the nearby Sarcheshmeh copper mines complex known as
Nicico. There are many monumental and historical buildings in this city
including: castles, water reservoirs, caravanserais, arcades, bazaars, towers,
mansions, natural freezers, temples, shrines, hills, mills and archaeological
sites.

86.
Kirmán is the name of a province and a city which is  provincial capital and
located in southeastern Iran. The city lies on a sandy plain, 5,738 feet (1,749
metres) above sea level, under barren rocky hills. Surrounded by mountains
on the north and east, it has a cool climate and frequent sandstorms in the
autumn and spring. The population is mostly Persian-speaking Muslims,
with a Zoroastrian minority. Kirmán is the largest carpet-exporting centre of
Iran. In the mid-1970s an industrial centre was established that included a
cement plant, a textile factory, and a cold-storage plant. There is a
thermoelectric plant; water is brought by qanats (underground aqueducts)
from the surrounding hills. Kermán is linked by road to Bandar ʿAbbás and
Mashhad. It also has an airport.  Kermán city was probably founded by
Ardashír I (reigned 224-241 CE) of the Sásániyán dynasty.   Under the
Ṣafavids, who took control in 1501, it came to be known as Kermán and was
made capital of a province. The city was sacked by the Uzbeks in 1509 but
was quickly rebuilt. Declining Ṣafavid power in the 17th and early 18th
centuries allowed Kermán to be attacked and occupied by Afghan tribesmen
in 1720. Kirmán was rebuilt under Fatḥ-ʿAlí Sháh, the founder of the Qájár
dynasty (ruled 1797-1834). Kermán province is bounded by the provinces
of Fárs on the west, Yazd on the north, South Khurásán on the northeast,


Sístán va Balúchistán on the east, and Hormozgán on the south. It includes
the southern part of the central Iranian desert, the Dasht-i Lút. The southern
Lút is relatively dry and saline, while in the east are sand dunes and in the
west, wind-carved ridges 180–240 feet (55-70 metres) high, called kalút.
Elsewhere are wind-eroded cuestas, locally called shahr lút. The Halíl Rúd
is the only river of importance. In the lowest depressions, the dry heat of
summer is unsurpassed anywhere in the world, but most of the upland
valleys enjoy a pleasant climate. The upland regions produce cereals, cotton,
sugar beets, oilseeds, fruit, and vegetables.  In the warm region below 3,600-
4,500 feet (1,100-1,370 metres), rice, corn (maize), henna, and fruits are
grown. Kermán exports a large percentage of the world’s pistachios. Gum
tragacanth is also gathered. Animal products are chiefly wool and kork, a
kind of soft wool used in Kermán shawls. The province is rich in minerals
such as copper, coal, chromium, lead, zinc, uranium, and aluminum, but
difficulty of access has restricted mining on a large scale. Springs of crude
oil have been found in the Kermán region. A gas pipeline from Bandar
ʿAbbás to Kermán and a network for the transmission of electricity have
helped the development of industry in the province. Besides Kermán, the
other towns of note are Báft, Bam, Rafsanján, Shahr-i Bábak, and Sirjan.
Area province, 69,821 square miles (180,836 square km). Pop. (2006) city,
515,114; province, 2,652,413. (Extracted from  Encyclopaedia Britannica).
87.
Falsafí:   The persecution and slaying of Bahá'ís was one of the daily
activities of the clerics, monarchs and rulers of the Qájár dynasty (1789 –
1925). Mohammad Shah (was born on 5 January 1808 and died on 5
September 1848), the third king in the Qájár dynasty; he reigned from 1834
– 1848. The Cause of God which was instituted by the Báb was born during
his reign. However, it was during the reign of Naser al-Din Shah (born on
16 July 1831 was assassinated on 1 May 1896),  who reigned forty eight
years (5 September 1848 – 1 May 1896), the Báb was martyred, Bahá’u’lláh
was imprisoned, and their followers were massacred in large numbers,
waves of persecution proceeded one after another. During the rule of Reza
Shah (from 16 December 1925 – 16 September 1941 ) who was the first
king in the Pahlavi dynasty, persecution and slaying of Bahá’ís continued.
The only difference with the past was that the government was not a party
to the slaying of Bahá’ís.  However, as a whole, persecution of Bahá'ís was
not one of the political objectives of Riza Shah's era, as his main goal was
to limit the influence of religious clerics. It was during his reign that the
notion of millat [national identity] acquired its roots, and to some degree,
the religious identity of ummat [body of believers] gave its place to millat.
That is, the country was progressing along the direction that every citizen


considered himself an Iranian without concern for religious belief, political
orientation or tribal affiliation. However, in the years after Riza Shah's
demise, gradually that policy was forgotten and, once more, the idea of
Ummat Islami [Body of Muslims] entered the political discourse. The
founding of Fadáíyan-i Islam [Devotees of Islam] took place after Riza Shah
was deposed and was an effort to combat leftist tendencies in Iran. After
Riza Shah, the government and Mohammad Riza Shah’s (he was the son of
Reza Shah reigned from 1941-1979) policies returned back to the path of
“Ummat Islami.”  With Riza Shah's exit, the clerics' long-held hatred of the
Bahá'ís once again reared up its head and of course it was extended to other
minority religions as well.  In reality, the widespread perfidious belief that
"any non-Islamic idea is a creation of foreigners" stemmed from the minds
of the mullás. Through this method, they wanted to call "anti-Iranian" and
"foreign" everything that was not Islamic or might prevent them from
acquiring power through Shi`ih religious pretexts. This deceitful "Othering",
and spreading the seeds of sedition and enmity, not only engulfed religious
minorities,  but also progressive nationalistic parties.
Beginning of 1955 Opposition to Bahá'ís:   After the 28 Murdád (19
August 1953) coup d'état, which brought back Muḥammad Riḍá Shah back
to power, the mullás insisted on their significant share in the revolt in his
support in order for him to come back to power. Subsequently the Shah was
forced to reward them, one of which was  to give in to the request of mullás
for the suppression of Bahá'ís. Two years after the coup d'état in 1953, due
to the pressure from clerics and in particular Áyatu’llah Burújirdí, with the
clear support from the Sháh a campaign against Bahá’ís started. A preacher
by the name Ḥujjatu'l-Islam Muḥammad -Taqí Falsafí, started his sermons
of hate from the pulpit of Masjid-i Sháh (Mosque of Sháh) against the
Bahá'ís.  In parallel with these sermons, a brutal attack on the lives of Bahá'ís
started and the government confiscated Bahá'í properties in every city. In
Ṭihrán, in front of the cameras of both foreign and domestic reporters,
General [Nader] Bátmánqulích, the chief of staff of the Iranian army, along
with [General] Taymúr Bakhtíyár, the military commander of Ṭihrán, took
pickaxes and  struck symbolically the dome of the National Bahá'í Centre
and stared the process of demolishing the dome of the National Bahá'í
Centre. For many years afterwards, that building was impounded by the
military and used for its own command centre.  On the first day of  the month
of fasting, Ramaḍán (23 April 1955) hate sermons started and the
destruction of the dome of the National Bahá'í centre started on 7 May 1955
and similarly the House of the Báb in Shiraz was partially destroyed. All
Bahá'í gatherings and institutions reduces their activities and large


gatherings stopped. Large number of Bahá'ís employed by the  government
were dismissed. This cooperation of the  government and the clergy to take
action against Bahá'ís took place almost two decades earlier than the Islamic
Revolution of Iran.  The late Muḥammad Riḍá Shah could not foresee the
future that through empowering the clergy and persecution of the loved ones
of God eventually he was striking at the root of his sovereignty.

Sarsaz is a reference to the profession of this person under consideration;
