89.
Buq‘at’ul-Khaḍrá is the appellation or the name of the Bahá’í cemetery in
Yazd which dates back to the time of Bahá’u’lláh. Originally Bahá’ís used
to bury their dead in Muslim cemeteries. After a while, Muslims refused to
allow the Bahá’ís to bury their dead in their cemetery as they regarded the
Bahá’ís unclean. Eventually, Bahá’ís were allowed to have their own
cemetery in Yazd which assumed the name “Buq‘at’ul-Khaḍrá” which
means green spot.
90.
Yazíd ibn Muʿáwíyah ibn Abi Sufíyán (Arabic: ﯾﺰﯾﺪ ﺑﻦ ﻣﻌﺎوﯾﺔ ﺑﻦ أﺑﻲ ﺳﻔﯿﺎن),
romanized: Yazíd son of  Muʿáwíyah son of Abú Sufíyān; ( 646 – 683A.D.),
commonly known as Yazíd-I, was the second caliph of the Umayyad
Caliphate. He ruled from April 680 until his death in November 683. He is
despised by the Shi‘ih due to his action which led to the martyrdom of Imam
Husayn. Also he is known to have deviated from the earlier practises of
Islamic rulers.
Yazíd was the son of Muʿáwíyah who inherited his father's rule. He was the
second of the dynastic rulers at the beginning of the Islamic era. His father
Muʿáwíyah had obtained power over the Muslim community through deceit
and treachery. During the last days of his reign he made the people pledge
their support for his son Yazíd. Thereupon he installed him as his successor
at an official ceremony.
Yazíd had inherited all the qualities of his character and the manner
according to which rule the nations of Islam from his father, Muʿáwíyah, all
that is, except for one thing: whereas, Muʿáwíyah governed by trickery and
deceit, compromised with the demands of Islam even to the point of building
mosques; Yazíd however committed the unseemly acts. Throughout his life
and especially during his period of rule, became notorious for the killing the
offspring of the Prophet of God and committed numerous other crimes at
the tragedy of Karbalá.
The most abominable of these was ordering the murder of Imam Ḥusayn,
his family and a group of his supporters, during the first year of his rule, and
to make captive the family of Imam Ḥusayn.   Moreover, in the second year


of his rule an event known as Harrah took place which was the scene of
further atrocities on his part. After four years of government Yazíd left this
world.
91.
Dr. Sulaymán Birjís was a physician in Kashan who was attacked and
killed by known assailants because of his Faith. He was born in 1276 Shamsi
(1897) in a Bahá’í family in Káshán. His father, Ya‘qúb Birjís, was one of
the famous physicians of Káshán; When he was in Ṭihrán and in the office
of his uncle who was also a physician started as an apprentice to learn medical
practise of his time. While in Ṭihrán he came into contact with the Bahá’ís and
became one of the of the followers of the Faith.
Ya‘qúb Birjís’s son, Sulaymán Birjís attended the school “Vahdat-i Bashar”
which was established and run by the Bahá’ís of Káshán. One of the reasons
for the fame of this school was that the teachers were familiar with English
and French languages. Young Sulaymán when was nineteen years old
travelled to Ṭihrán to attend “Dar’ul-Funún” for studying medicine, and
after completing his studies and apprenticeship he returned to Káshán to
work at his father’s medical practice. Dr. Sulaymán Birjís was known to
respond to visit house-bound patients and he was assisting people who could
not afford the medicine, giving them free of charge the medical care they
needed; he was very popular in Káshán because of his care for the needy.
Apart from his profession, he  had a good knowledge of the Torah, Gospel
and the Qur’an. One of the reasons which led to his murder was the
threatening preaching from the pulpit by Shaykh Muḥammad Khálisí-zádih.
He was very radical Islamist preacher born in Kázimayn, Iraq. He had a
good connection with Aḥmad Shah but opposed Riḍá Shah and because of
his opposition to the Court of the king, he was sent on exile several times to
different places. He resided in Káshán from 1322 (1943) onwards. Another
person who played a key role in the murder of Dr. Birjís was another preacher
by the name of ‘Ali Akbar Turbatí. Torbatí was sent in 1326 (1947) by
Áyyatu’llah Burújirdí to Káshán.
He was inciting the fanatic Muslims to spill the blood of Bahá’ís and was
telling them such action was consistent with the teachings of Islam. This led
to the action of eight youths who caused the martyrdom of  Dr. Birjís.
On Friday, 14th Bahman 1328 (3rd February, 1950) at 8:30 am two
individuals by the names ‘Abbás Tavassuli and ‘Ali Naqí-Pur went to the
office of Dr. Birjís where he was practising medicine and asked him to come
and visit an old dying women who needed medical attention, they took Dr.
Sulaymán Birjís to a house under this false pretext and then with the help of
Ḥáj Muḥammad Rasúl-zádih, Drúdgar, Riḍá Gulsurkhi and three others they


executed a pre-meditated murder of an innocent man. As soon as Dr.
Sulaymán Birjís reached the place, eight individuals fell on him started
beating him up, after that they cut him into pieces with eighty-one knife
strokes then they dragged him on the roof top of the house and dropped him
down.
After the incidence (according to their plan), four of the killers went to the
police station and confessed to the killing and they were arrested. Then at
the instigation of some people like Shaykh ‘Abdu’l-Karim Turbatí Waez,
large crowds of mosques and bazaars gathered around the police station to
force the release of the murders.
The killing of people under religious pretexts, can only be searched in the
period of barbarism and the dark ages of the Middle Ages, and in the present
period, progressive humanity is ashamed of such operations. Religious strife
and killings are practiced only to secure the dominance of those who believe
in prejudice. Some people have indicated that Fadáíyyán Islam with the
leadership of Navváb-i Ṣafaví has also been involved in this plot.
The trial of the murderers and preliminary investigation along with the
alleged offenders were sent to Ṭihrán. Despite the voluntary confession of
all eight individuals, and outcome should have been punishment according
to the law of the country, but they were set free at the end.
Áyyat’ulláh Burujirdí, Bihbahání and Faizí started exerting their maximum
pressure for the release of these eight individuals. The Bahá’í community
by writing to the Shah, Prime Minister, sent  petition to Minister of Justice
and the Chief of Police for justice. But such a plea was ignored and the Prime
Minister was dismissed within two weeks. Then Mansúr and Razm Árá
became prime ministers one after the other and they rejected the plea of the
Bahá’í community. Navváb-i Ṣafaví came to the court room where trial was in
progress and caused confusion and court proceedings closed and went on
recess.
At the end, the court on 26 Shahrivar 1329 (17 September 1950), despite the
confession of the murderers and countless witnesses, were exonerated and
allowed to go free.

93.
Tone and manner of debate:  In the revelation of Bahá’u’lláh, teaching the
Cause of God with wisdom is the duty of every Bahá’í. In Iran about a century
ago, every individual tried to identify fair minded seekers of truth who were
sincere and then started entering into conversation with them on  spiritual
matters. That was the general rule. However, once in a while, there were
people who had double face and intended to bring harm to Bahá’ís. This was
very rare, but it could happen. ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí has tried to explain
one of such instances. In such cases, the tone of the debate between Bahá’í
believers and their adversaries, who had no intention of accepting the truth
under any circumstances, was formal but polite and at times could be
described as confrontational; and this was nearly a century ago. Talking about
one’s religion and politics are frowned at in the western world today and
therefore people try to avoid it altogether; this is mainly due to the fact that it
could lead to unpleasant confrontation. Documenting such debates, as has
been done by ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí, is historically of some value,
particularly when it covers the social norms of the time which were to some
extent different than those of toady. We should not judge such debates by our
current standards. This story is not cited for the purpose of emulating it today.
94.
Áqá Mírzá Ḥasan Núsh-Ábádí:   Áqá Mírzá Ḥasan Núsh-Ábádí was one
of the capable teachers of the Cause of God in Iran. As his name implies he
was born, and had his early childhood, in Núsh-Ábád, one of the villages
near Kashan. The full story of his life is covered in Maṣábíḥ-i-Hidáyat
[MBH], vol. 6, pp. 96-167. He was born in 1270 Shamsi (1892). The name
of his father was Luṭf-‘Alí and the name of his mother was Ḥalímih Khátún
and both believed in the New Revelation of God. Unfortunately due to the
epidemic of cholera both parents passed away when he was 12 years old, he
became an orphan while he had no brothers or sisters to look after him. He
started travelling with Mírzá Mihdí Akhaván Ṣafá (one of the teachers of the
Cause of God) as his assistant and then after learning from him how to teach
the Cause of God, he started to travel for teaching the Cause independently.
In 81 BE he travelled to the Holy Land for pilgrimage and met the Guardian.
He has written a book: Baráhin (Proofs) nearly 800 pages which has not
been printed or published, and also Rashahát ‘Irfán (The sprinkles of the
knowledge of God). He married Thábitih Khánum the daughter of Áqá
Muḥammad Thábit Sharqí. He participated in Singapore conference in
115BE and in 1340 Shamsi (1961) passed away in Shiraz.
95.
Arák:    Arák, previously Sulṭánábád, city, capital of Markazí province,
north-western Iran. It was founded as Sulṭánábád in 1808 by the Qájár ruler
Fatḥ-ʿAlí Sháh. By the end of the century, it had become an important centre
of carpet production. During the reign of Riḍá Sháh Pahlavi (1925–41), the


local name Arák was adopted as the official name. In the late 20th century
the city developed into a major industrial centre, producing steel,
aluminium, petrochemical products, vehicles, and heavy machinery. Pop.
(2011) 484,200. (Extracted from Encyclopædia Britannica, Inc.)
Arák has offered a number of martyrs to the Faith. In here we will mention
only the Seven Martyrs of Arák which took place in 1916. This presentation
is based on what has been recorded in the book “The Life of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá
by Muḥammad-‘Alí Faizi [LAB]”:  Jináb-i Mírzá ‘Alí-Akbar Barár, the son
of Mullá Ḥusayn was from Kashan and was living in Sulṭán-Ábád who was
martyred two year after the martyrdom of the Four Martyrs of Arak in the
hands of those who worked for Ḥájí Siyyid Muḥammad Báqir and Áqá
Ḍíyá’. Mírzá ‘Alí-Akbar grew up in this place and went on pilgrimage to
visit ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. After his return, he continued with his profession which
was trade, progressed well and obtained much wealth which attracted the
enmity of the local people. During WWI when the Russian forces had
reached near Sulṭán-Ábád and camped there, security was non-existent, the
enemies of the Cause of God took advantage of the situation and on the night
of 17th of Rabi‘uth-Thani 1334 (22 February 1916) murdered Mírzá ‘Alí-
Akbar and six members of his family. Those who were martyred were as
follows:
i.
Mírzá ‘Alí-Akbar Barár who was sixty five years old.
ii.
Maryan khanum – his wife
iii.
‘Abdu’l-Ḥusayn – his son of 12 years old
iv.
Mírzá Áqá – his son who was nine years old
v.
‘Abdu’llah – his son who was six years old
vi.
‘Abdu’r-Raḥím who was an infant of forty days old
vii.
Khurshíd khanum the sister of Maryam khanum who was fourteen
years old
96.
Mírzá Mihdí Akhaván Ṣafá:    Mírzá Mihdí Akhaván Ṣafá was one of the
travel teachers in Iran. The story of his life has been covered in Maṣábíḥ-i-
Hidáyat [MAH], vol  4, pp. 4-91. His father was Muḥammad Báqir who
lived in Yazd and had many children of which three girls and six boys, all
together nine children. Mírzá Mihdí was one of the nine who reached the
age of young adults. Some of these children became believers in the new
Faith and some followed their old religion of Islam. Mírzá Mihdí was one
them who became a believer. Some of the siblings became also martyrs in
the events of Yazd in 1321 AH(1903). Akhaván Ṣafá is the title that ‘Abdu’l-
Bahá granted to this family. The trade that he learned was weaving. He
survived the atrocities of 1903 in Yazd and Travelled to Ṭihrán and joined


the class and teaching group of Ṣadru’uṣ-Ṣudúr. After he finished the
teaching class, started travel teaching and travelled in Iran for the sake of
the promotion of the Cause of God. He travelled to the Holy land and visited
‘Abdu’l-Bahá. He returned and continued with his service.  He passed away
in 1337 AH (1919).
97.
Ḥájí Siyyid Javád Surúsh:  There were two  brothers in Yazd who were
among the earlier believers of the Faith from the time of the Báb; they were
sons of Siyyid Surúsh. Their names were Siyyid Ibrahím and Áqá Siyyid
Muḥammad-‘Alí. Áqá Siyyid Muḥammad-‘Alí had four children, two boys
and two girls. The names of the male children were Siyyid Javád and Siyyid
‘Alí. Both of them, after obtaining permission from Bahá’u’lláh, travelled
to ‘Akká and visited His holy presence and with His permission made ‘Akká
as their place of residence. Ḥájí Siyyid Javád married Gohar khánum, the
sister of Munírih khanum, the wife of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. The daughter of Ḥájí
Siyyid Javád, Bi Bi Riḍváníyyih, married Áqá Mírzá Ḥusayn, the son of
Ḥájí ‘Abdu’r-Raḥím Qannád.  According to the instructions of ‘Abdu’l-
Bahá and after the ascension of Bahá’u’lláh, Ḥájí Siyyid Javád and his
family moved to Port Sai‘d. (Táríkh Amrí Yazd, [TAY] p. 152)

98.
Jamál Burújirdí:  Jamál Burújirdí was a learned cleric and mullá who
became a Bahá’í and visited Bahá’u’lláh and ‘Abdu’l-Bahá in
Adrianople and ‘Akká. He was imprisoned in Iran for sometime. He was
known by the name Ismu’llah Jamál. He received several tablets from
Bahá’u’lláh. He was very proud, expected Bahá’ís to pay high respect for
him, and to provide him with means for a comfortable living like the
clergy class in Islam. However, his demise occurred after the ascension
of Bahá’u’lláh and during the time of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. Covenant Breakers
pampered him and he thought he could make a division in the ranks of
the believers to his own advantage. Eventually, he was sanctioned and
Bahá’ís paid no attention to him. He died in misery and poverty.
99.
Mírzá Ḥaydar-‘Alí Isfahani (1834-1920). He was a native of Isfahan and
his father was Mírzá Ḥusayn, a merchant. He accepted the message of the
Báb when he was rather young, was considered as a Bábí for a while then
he travelled to Adrianople and visited Bahá’u’lláh. He was there for seven
months then he was sent to Istanbul to facilitate the travel of friends. His
service to the Cause started from here and continued for half a century.
Bahá’u’lláh towards the final year of His stay in Adrianople sent him to
Egypt to teach the Cause with wisdom, but it did not take him long before
the Iranian Consul General to arrest him, to deliver him to the Egyptian


authorities; he was introduced to them as a rebellious and dangerous
element. He was exiled to Khartum in Sudan. He was there for thirteen
years and after  gaining his freedom, he was granted the permission to visit
Bahá’u’lláh in Ákká after which he travelled back to Iran and was a
successful travel teacher. Once again he visited Ákká in the early years
during the time of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. He again travelled to Iran and
strengthened the understanding of the friends about the Covenant. When
he was old and fragile he travelled to the Holy Land and resided there. He
was known as the “Angel of Carmel”. His famous publication is Bihjatu’ṣ-
Ṣudúr. (The Delight of Hearts) and Dalail’ul-Irfán (Proofs of the knowledge
of God). For further reading see Eminent Bahá’ís by Ḥasan Balyuzi, [EMB]
pp. 237-250: “Angel of Mount Carmel” He passed away in 1338 AH (1920).
His life story is covered in Maṣábíḥ-i-Hidáyat, [MAH] vol  1, pp. 1-92.
100.
Qábil-i Ábádihyí:  Qábil-i Ábádihyí is a successful travel teacher from
Ábádih (in the Province of Fars on the road between Shiraz-Isfahan) which
is not too far from Shiraz. He was a poet as well. He lived during the time
‘Abdu’l-Bahá and Shoghi Effendi. The story of his life is covered in
Maṣábíḥ-i-Hidáyat [MAH], vol. 2, pp. 158-189. He was borne in one of
the villages of Ábádih in 1280 AH(1863). His given name was ‘Abbás.
The name of his father was Ghulam-‘Alí. Mírzá ‘Abbás had his early and
basic education in Ábádih and when he was nineteens years old came into
contact with the Bahá’í Faith and believed in Bahá’u’lláh. Despite the
persecution in Ábádih, he continued to teach the Cause of God. He selected
the pen name “Qábil” for himself which numerically is equivalent to
‘Abbás. In 1337 A.H.(1919) along with 18 others, a total of nineteen
Bahá’ís , from Ábádih went on pilgrimage to visit ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. He has
received several tablets from ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. During the time of the
Guardian he was very active in the field of teaching. He  passed away in
1315 Shamsi (1936).
101.
Mullá ‘Alí Ján & ‘Alavíyyih khanum:    Mullá ‘Alí Ján, entitled “The
lofty, the most exalted” is one of the radiant faces in the history of the
Bahá’í Faith. He was among the learned and from the rank of clergy who
accepted the Faith, a great teacher of the Cause of God and a martyr. He
also left a devoted family behind. Mullá ‘Alí Ján was borne in the village
of Riykandih which was twelve km from the town of Sárí (in the Province
of Mazandaran) in the year 1262 AH (1846). Sárí itself is not too far from
the location of the Shrine of the Shaykh Tabarsi where a great campaign
of devotion, bravery, self-sacrifice and endurance in the face of difficulties
took place. When this campaign was underway, he was a child of tender
age. The name of his father was Ja ‘far and that his mother was Áminih.


Soon he lost both parents. He received the elements of basic education at
home and then continued with advanced studies to become a priest. In
1284 AH (1868) he was convinced of the truth of the Faith.  What attracted
him initially to the Faith were the great prophecies that he read in the
Islamic literature about ‘Akká. He could not understand its reason at that
time. He investigated this matter and was led to the Bahá’í Faith. Then he
was convinced of the truth of the Faith. At this time he was studying in
Barfurúsh (Currently Bábul) which was not too far from Sárí. After
finishing his studies he became the priest (Imam Jum‘ih) of Máh-Furúzak.
He gradually started acquainting his followers to the precepts of the Bahá’í
faith. Then he started to teach the Faith openly as a result of which the
great majority of the inhabitants of the village of Máh-Furúzak became
Bahá’ís. About this time he married his cousin, ‘Alavíyyih khanum. The
progress of the Faith in the surrounding villages alarmed the authorities.
In  the year 1300 AH (1883) when persecution of Bahá’ís was underway
all over Iran, Mullá ‘Alí Ján, along with some other Bahá’ís from Máh-
Furúzak, were arrested and sent to Ṭihrán and were imprisoned. Finally he
was put to death on 21 Sha’bán 1300 (23 June 1883). He became one of
the heroes and outstanding martyrs of the Faith. Also his wife, ‘Alavíyyih
khanum, and family members were arrested and sent to Sárí and they were
humiliated and then released. They ransacked their house and their
properties. From this couple a devoted family followed. Buzurg ‘Alavíyan
is another martyr from this family who drank from the cup of martyrdom
during the start of the Islamic Revolution of Iran. (The story of his life is
covered in Maṣábíḥ-i-Hidáyat [MAH], vol. v, pp. 500-537). It should be
stressed that‘Alavíyyih khanum in the way of perfection and faith was not
much less than her illustrious husband.
102. Bakhtyárí:   Bakhtyárí, also spelled Bakhtiyárí, one of the two nomad tribes
of Iran; the second one is the Qashqáyí. The chiefs of Bakhtyárí have been
among the greatest tribal leaders in Iran and have long been influential in
Persian politics. The Bakhtyárí population of approximately 880,000
occupies roughly 25,000 square miles (65,000 square km) of plains and
mountains in western Iran. They speak the Lurí dialect of Persian and are
Shíʿit Muslims. Sedentarized Bakhtyárís live in towns and in many villages
in Chahár  Maḥál, in the Farídan district down to Isfahan, and in Khuzistan
down to Ahváz. The nomads and some sedentary people live in the tribal
territory, called the “Bakhtyárí country”, an area of roughly 75,000 km2
stretching from the Dez river, Shúshtar, and Rám Hurmuz on the west to
Dárán and the outskirts of Shahr-i Kurd on the east.


The Bakhtyárí nomads move between a summer abode (yiyláq) in the high
mountains (summit, Zardakúh 4,548 m) and a winter abode (garmsír) in the
western foothills adjoining the Khuzistan plain. The ecological boundary
between the two zones coincides roughly with the course of the Áb-i Bázuft.
Thus the Bakhtyárí country falls into two different administrative provinces:
Chahár  Maḥál, where the summer quarters lie, and Khuzistan, in which the
winter quarters are included.
Many of the Bakhtyárí are nomadic, pastoral tent dwellers who are
dependent on their flocks of sheep, goats, and cattle. They migrate their
flocks 150 miles each year between their winter pastures in the plains and
the summer pastures of the mountains. Agricultural products are mainly
obtained by trade or as tax from dependent villages. There has been some
urbanization among the Bakhtyárí resulting from the oil industry.
The Bakhtyárí are divided into two main tribal groups, the Chahár Lang
(“Four Legs”) and the Haft Lang (“Seven Legs”). Each of these groups is
controlled by a single chiefly family that holds extensive political power and
owns sizable herds and farmlands. The position of khán, or paramount
leader, of the Bakhtyárí is held for two years by the chief of the Haft Lang,
with the chief of the Chahár Lang as his Ílbig, or deputy. For the next two
years the two chiefs exchange their posts with each other.
A factor which further strengthened the power of the Bakhtyárí kháns
(chiefs) was the presence of British firms in Khuzistan from the end of the
nineteenth century onward. The opening of the Lynch road through the south
of the Bakhtyárí country in 1897 was followed by the discovery and
extraction of petroleum at Masjid-i Sulaymán and later at other places in the
tribe’s garmsír. These developments deprived several thousand nomads of
traditional pastures but brought a bonanza to the greater chiefs. For their
undertaking to maintain the security of the oil installations, these chiefs were
rewarded with 5 percent of the shares of the First Exploration Company, and
as a result became entitled to part of the Anglo-Iranian Oil Company’s
profits for twenty-five years and to substantial compensation after the
company’s withdrawal.
Bakhtyárí chiefs have long been influential in Iranian politics. In 1909
Bakhtyárí tribesmen under the Haft Lang chief Sardar Assad captured
Ṭihrán in their successful campaign to press for constitutional reforms in
Iran. Many Bakhtyárí have held prominent public offices since then,
including governorates of provinces and important ministerial posts in the
central government.


Bakhtyárí women have long enjoyed a high degree of freedom that is
atypical of Muslim women elsewhere. The daughters of tribal leaders are
normally given at least an elementary education.
(Summarized from  Encyclopaedia Iranica & Encyclopaedia Britannica )
103. Shahr-i-Kurd:  It is a City in Chahár  Maḥál and Bakhtyárí Province. In
fact Shahr-i Kurd is the capital city of the Province. It is the largest city in
the Province, and is 90 km away from Iran's third largest city, Isfahan. This
city is located to the northeast of this province, and is situated 543 km south
of Ṭihrán. Shahr-i-Kurd is in the midst of a plain surrounded by mountains.
In the 7th Century AH Shahr-i-Kurd and its outskirts came under the rule of
the Atábakán-i-Fárs and Luristán. It was in this area that a sentry post was
set up to control both passenger traffic and see to their need. The ancient
name of Shahr-i-Kurd has been heard since Zandiyyih era; however,
according to the findings of archaeologists, and specifically with regard to
the ancient hills around the city is dating back to millenniums B. C.  Thanks
to its special roadway location, Shahr-i-Kurd was used as a police station by
Atábakán of Fars and Luristán. Currently, the people living in Shahr-i Kurd
are of three major backgrounds: Bakhtyárí, Qashqáyi and indigenous
Persian speakers of Shahr-i Kurd; however, the people of Shahr-i Kurd
speak Persian as a lingua franca. Shahr-i Kurd is assumed to be first
developed by nomadic farmers because it encompasses vast meadows and
several springs. It was founded by the immigrants from Isfahan, its suburbs,
and the villages around Shahr-i Kurd, most of whom started to run
businesses such as agriculture, horticulture, hat making, felt making, oil and
wool trading, and other industries.
Shahr-i Kurd enjoys a semi-moist and moderate climate with moderate
summers and very cold winters. The annual average temperature in Shahr-i
Kurd is 11°C. Although the humidity level is moderate or high in winter, the
amount of rainfall is close to zero in planting seasons, except for April and
May.
Source:  https://www.itto.org/iran/city/Shahrekord/
104. Abu’l-Faḍl Raḥmání:   Abu’l-Faḍl Raḥmání is the brother of Jináb-i Hádí
Raḥmání who was a counsellor and the manger of Naw Nahálán Company.
Both of them are sons of Áqá Ja‘far Raḥmání who was a native of Fars
Province. Áqá Mírzá Ja‘far Raḥmání was the son of Mírzá Hádí Shirazi. The
father and son travelled to Ishq-Ábád  in 1304 AH (1886) (Notice that the
name of Mírzá Ja‘far’s father as well as that of his son was Hádí). At the


time of his arrival in Ishq-Ábád,  Mírzá Ja‘far did not confess the Bahá’í
belief, neither did his father.  Ḥájí Mírzá Hádí was a devout Muslim and
whenever and wherever heard the call to prayer "Adhán” he did the same
with a loud voice. Ḥájí Mírzá Hádí and Áqá Ja‘far became engaged in trade
in Ishq-Ábád. It did not take long before Ḥájí Mírzá Hádí contracted a
disease and became very sick and passed away. After the death of his father,
Áqá Ja‘far travelled to Khujand (now in Tajikistan) and established himself
there. Then travelled to the Holy Land and built the Eastern traveller’s
Hospice, during the time of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, which served as a lodge for
eastern pilgrims well into the time of the Guardian. It still stands and serves
as pilgrim’s house which receives visitors. Áqá Ja‘far has received tablets
from ‘Abdu’l-Bahá (see Tarikh Ishq-Ábád pp. 341-342). Also the life
history of Mírzá Ja‘far Raḥmání, the son of Ḥájí Mírzá Hádí is written by
his son Jináb-i Hádí Raḥmání in Áhang Badi‘ – year no. 28 , no. 11-12, year
1352, pp. 21-26.
105. Dr. ‘Alí- Murád Dávúdí was one of the members of the National Spiritual
Assembly in the decade before the Islamic Revolution of Iran and was its
Secretary. He was a professor of philosophy who made himself retired to
devote his time to the service of the Cause. After the 1979 Islamic
Revolution and in its early years, he was walking in a park where he was
kidnapped and nothing was heard from him after that. Without any doubt he
was killed and he is one of the recent martyrs of the Cause of God. Apart
from his self-sacrifice and outstanding services to the Cause of God, he was
a true scholar and the author of several books one of which is “Divinity and
Revelation”.
106. Naw Nahálán Company: It is a trust company established by the Bahá’í
Community of Iran which was inspired by  the Master. Its origin was in the
city of Qazvin. The story of the start of this company from its beginnings in
the city of Qazvin to its developments as reported in the book “Yárán-i
Qazvin [YAQ]” is being translated and summarize below. The
aforementioned story in turn has used several articles in the magazine
Payam Bahá’í numbers 266,  270 and 205; and also Akhbár Amrí (Bahá’í
News), the publication of the National Spiritual Assembly of Iran, year 46,
annual number 4 and 5 for the solar year 1346 and 1967:
Naw Nahálán was establish with the efforts and initiative of Muḥammad
Labíb, a school teacher in the Bahá’í School of Tavakkul in Qazvin, and
a teacher in Bahá’í Children Classes. The humble beginning of this
company started with the pocket money which the children were


receiving from the their parents and they could buy some coupons,
whoever accumulated enough coupons worth one share of the company
they would exchange the coupons for a share and in this manner such
children would become shareholders of the  company. This company had
been approved by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. To confer blessing upon it, He Himself
contributed two gold coins and symbolically He became a shareholder as
well. The capital of the company continuously had upward trend in 1336
AH (1918) it was less than $100. It became a habit that parents would
purchase share as gifts in the name of their children and as a support for
them when children performed well in their studies and other events.
‘Abdu’l-Bahá in a tablet has made some remarks about this company,
and in the same tablet He asks God to make this company of the Bahá’í
children well rooted and let it continue into the future … There are other
tablets (refer to the tablet MKT3, p. 265). There are letters from the
Guardian for this company as well in which he prays for the success of
the company and the Bahá’í children who supported it. A few decades
later, this company had grown in size substantially and had become a
company with national outlook with sizeable capital. After Islamic
Revolution of Iran, one of the first things they did  was to occupy and
confiscate the company, though the staff still were working there under
their control and later they confiscated it altogether. It is mentioned that
Abdu’l-Bahá once said that this company belongs to me. The capital of
the company in 1979 was close to $10 million and the number of the
employees of the company was fifty five. It had a board of director
consisting of seven individuals and a general manager. Jináb-i Hádí
Rahmání was the last general manager of this company. Since it was also
a business, it was lending money and assisting individuals who were in
difficult situation. (Yárán Gazvin, [YAQ] pp. 631 - 636 )

107. Prayer by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá quoted in the book: “ ‘Áqibat Bikhayr”, [ABK]  p.
181 and also in the Muntakhabát-i Makátíb ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, [MMA] vol. 1, in
Persian, no. 22, page, 49. This collection of the Writings of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá
has been translated and published as a book called : “Selections from the
Writings of ‘Abdul-Baha” [SWA] tablet number 22 in SWA.

108. In the Bahá’í Faith we have prayers for protection, healing, and several
other occasional prayers. There are similar prayers in Islam and particularly
in Shí‘h, Islam, which have been written by revered Shí‘h Imams. It became
a habit for some of the faithful believers in Islam, who were illiterate, to
carry written versions of such prayers with them and particularly prayers
for protection for children. These prayers were written, folded and placed
in tiny bags and worn by the support of an arm band or worn like a necklace
around one’s neck. The Báb established procedures for writing His prayers
and further restricted the manner by which His revealed prayers were to be
written. Bahá’u’lláh further restricted the use of such practise except by His
permission. In the age of enlightenment, one might think that a piece of
paper on which something is written has no power of enactment. ‘Abdu’l-
Bahá explains that the power of faith, belief and reliance upon the Words
of God, exerts tremendous forces. This also holds true when we say any
prayer; the physical aspect of saying a prayer has minimal effects, however,
when it is strengthened by the power of faith it can produce wonderful
results. (Refer to a Table from ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, Amr va Khalq, vol. 4, pp. 68-
69 – 128 BE; [AVK, vol. 4, pp. 68-69])
Haykal or Temple is nothing but a reference to a shape and in this case the
shape of human body. It is possible to write the revealed Writings in a
manner which at the end produces an image similar to the shape of a human
being consisting of one head, two hands and two legs very much like five
pointed star in which symmetry has been preserved but each point has a
different size. Writing the revealed verses in this manner is one of the
features of the religion of the Báb and we have many images of this nature
which were produced by Him. A copy of two images of this kind have been
placed at the end of this endnote.  These images have been  produced by the
Báb Himself which are taken from the book “ ‘Ahd-i-Ala” by Abu’l-Qasim
Afnan.  In the first year of His revelation, the Báb instructed His male
followers to write his Writings in the form of Haykal (Temple) and wear
them on their chest. He instructed the same thing for female believers except


that He instructed them to write down His Writings in the form of a circle.
In other words, the circle form for females and the temple form for male
believers.
In particular there is a prayer for protection which is written in the form of
Haykal (Temple) and is known as the prayer of temple (Du’a-i-Haykal).
Refer to Illustration number 35. This is a copy of the “Prayer of Haykal”
which has been produced by the Báb; this includes writing as well as
symbols. The text of such Haykal can be examined in Athrar’ul-’Athar, vol.
4, p. 115. The first  few sentences of this Tablet (in Arabic) are as follows:

One more thing to remember is that the Báb commanded His believers to
transcribe His Writings with the utmost care in the way of calligraphy, and
superior paper, ink, etc. As an example refer to the Persian Bayán, chapter
14 of vahid 3; 3:14. This should be applicable to the reproduction of the
prayer of Haykal as well.
Also it should be remembered that apart from the prayer of haykal by the
Báb which is used for protection, there is a Tablet by Bahá’u’lláh with the
same name. In another Tablet, He gives detailed instructions how to write
the prayer, the kind of ink and paper to use for this purpose and how to fold
the paper. In the same Tablet, He confirms that if someone else to write the
prayer for anyone, one mithqál of gold should be given to him as a gift. There
are instructions regarding how to say the prayer. This Tablet which is
addressed to Zaynu’l-Muqarrabín has been published in “Muḥaḍirát, vol. 1,
page 416” by ‘Abdu’l-Ḥámid-i Ishráq-Khávari.
These prayers which are short should not be mistaken with two other Tablets.
One is the Tablet of Hayakil or Temples which is an explanation by
Bahá’u’lláh about a Tablet of the Báb revealed for Asadu’lláh Dayyán. The
second one is the Tablet of Haykal which is also called Súrih-i-Haykal which
is a long Tablet. These two Tablets are not a part of the current  discussion.

109. Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl Gundulí:  (The story of his life is translated and
presented from Khátirát-i Málmírí, [KHM] pp. 35-37) He was the son-in-law
of Ḥájí Muḥammad Afshár (The author of the book: The Sea of the Knowledge
of God – Baḥr’ul-‘Irfan) who was a teacher of Cause and a firm believer during
the time of Bahá’u’lláh and then ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl was
a religious man and was adhering tenaciously to the precepts and teaching of
Islam.  It was now a few years that Ḥájí Muḥammad Afshár (his father-in-law)
had accepted the Faith but was keeping his belief hidden from him. Should he
have become aware of his acceptance of the Bahá’í Faith, there would have
been a great possibility that he would divorce his wife (the daughter of Ḥájí
Muḥammad Afshár).
In one year that  the Islamic month of Ramaḍan was in summer and the
afternoon weather in Yazd was very hot Ḥájí Muḥammad Táhir Málmiri relates:
“I was at the home of Áqá Muḥammad Riḍá Muḥammad-Ábádí Shahid; in the


afternoon, Ḥájí Muḥammad Afshár brought his son-in-law, who was fasting at
the time,  to the afore-mentioned house. Due to the heat of the air during daytime,
we were sitting in the basement. As soon as Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl arrived, he
lied down and covered himself up all-the-way from his toe to his head with his
abá and he tried to sleep. Then Ḥájí Muḥammad Afshár though was a believer
in the Faith started asking questions for the benefit of his-in-law so that he could
hear both the questions and their answers. I was answering some of these
questions. He was rejecting some and accepting some. This conversation and
debate took about four hours. Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl  was apparently awake
under the cover of abá and was listening to our conversation. It was about two
hours before sunset he, all of a sudden, removed the abá which was covering
him and requested for tea and in this manner he broke his fast despite the fact
that he was very much attached to the ordinances of Islam and strictly obeyed its
laws. In this way he accepted the Faith very quickly and passed across the bridge
and arrived at the garden of the acceptance of the New Revelation. He had an
office in the Caravanserai Khájih and was engaged in trading.
After accepting the Faith he started immediately to teach the cause of God
and openly announced his belief in the New Revelation. This caused some
people to make an attempt to kill him. They encouraged a person by the name
Javád Shekári to kill him when he was returning home from his office. So he
was awaiting for an opportune time until one day they came face to face with
each other and this place was not too far away from his house. Javád fired a
bullet into his belly. But God protected him, he was saved which was the
cause of astonishment of many people. Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl  approached
Jalálu’d-Dawlih, the governor, and complained. The government followed
up the matter but the assailant fled away thereby was not punished.
However, Ḥájí Muḥammad Ismá‘íl  increased his enkindlement after this
incidence which did not become the means for his despair. Rather, he made
his house as the place for Mashriqu’l-Adhkár and his house for a number of
years till the time of disturbances was the place of Mashriqu’l-Adhkár. He
himself, when midnight approached used to go and open the door and await
the arrival of the friends. The friends were engaged in prayers until early
morning hours in that house. Indeed this  blessed man was the cause of
Exhilaration and happiness of the friends and he had no thoughts or intention
save the exaltation of the Faith and its promotion. He had a good sense of
humor and had very good manners. He was always smiling when meeting
others be it a friend or a stranger. He did not have much knowledge but the
way he argued he could convince the learned. This continued until the
disturbances and intense atrocities in Yazd started and what he endured is
recorded in the Tarikh Shuhadyi Yazd.


110.
Tákur  is a village in Núr  County, Mazandaran Province, Iran. Mazandaran
forms the southern-most shores of the Caspian Sea. Tákur’s population is
currently about fifty families. It is located about twenty km to the west of the city
of Ámul and at about a distance of fifty km from the Caspian Sea.  The province
of Mazandaran has high mountain peaks in the south descending to the northern
coastal plain bordering the sea and covered with lots of vegetation and enjoys
good amounts of rain fall. Because of the dense sub-tropical jungles of the lower
parts of the province (a stark contrast to the dry desert conditions in much of the
rest of Iran), it was always a difficult area for invaders to penetrate. The
Zoroastrian royalty and nobility fled to these parts after the Arab Islamic invasion
of Iran in the seventh century.  Bahá'u'lláh's family have descended from the last
monarch of pre-Islamic Iran. Tákur was the ancestral home of Mírzá Buzurg, the
Father of Bahá’u’lláh. Mírzá Buzurg was the master of calligraphy who was
called the second Mír Imád. His good hand-writing and calligraphic capabilities
eased his way to the court of Fatḥ-ʻAlí S͟ háh. Mírzá Buzurg Núrí, rose in the ranks
of the civil servants to become the minister to a royal prince who was the
commander of the royal guards. He became later a vizier (minister).  He was given
the village of Tákur in the Núr region in lieu of salary and he built a fine mansion
there by the side of the Núr river as a family home. He worked for a time in the
service of Fatḥ-ʻAlí S͟ háh. Mírzá Buzurg was the son of Mírzá Riḍá-Qulí Big, son
of Mírzá ʻAbbás, son of Ḥájí Muḥammad-Riḍá Big, son of Áqá Muhammad-ʻAli,
son of Áqá Fak͟ hr, son of S͟ hahríyár-Ḥasan. Bahá’u’lláh spent some months in
Tákur during summers when the weather in Ṭihrán was very hot. Due to floods
in one year the house of Mírzá Buzurg was washed away and then it was rebuilt.

111. The following note has been written by Bahá’u’lláh when he was about six
or seven years old. Refer to the book: “ ‘Áqibat Bikhayr”,  pp. 192-193;
Payam Bahá’í, no 307, June 2005, pp. 20-21. Also another source is an article
in the Book “The Best-Beloved of the World”, written by Abu’l-Qásim
Afnán, page 180  states that He was eight years old when wrote that
letter/Tablet.

112. Mírzá Buzurg  and Ḥájí Mírzá Áqásí: The second Prime Minister of
Muḥammad shah, Ḥájí Mírzá Áqásí, was antagonistic to Mírzá Buzurg. One
reason which prompted his enmity was Mírzá Buzurg's particular friendship


with the Qá'im-Maqám, Mírzá Abu'l-Qásim of Farahán. In June 1835 the
Qá'im-Maqám was put to death by the order of Muhammad Shah. Mírzá
Buzurg wrote letters condemnatory of Ḥájí Mírzá Áqásí, which the Prime
Minister eventually encountered, and retaliated with force. He had Mírzá
Buzurg dismissed from the governorship of Burujird and Luristán. This post
had been given to him by the Qá'im-Maqám. Next, Ḥájí Mírzá Áqásí stopped
Mírzá Buzurg's annual allowance.  Manuscripts exist in his superb and
much-admired handwriting, in various collections both in and outside of
Iran. There is one such scroll in the International Archives of the Baháʼí
World Centre.
As for the history of the Faith in Tákur,  it was twice attacked by the
government troops and some of the Baháʼís were taken prisoner.
113. Seventeen or more  martyrs:   On 23 day of Ramaḍán 1308 (May 2,
1891) two believers from Yazd went to a mosque to see one of their friends.
The people in the mosque attacked them and took them to the government
quarters demanding their execution. Jalálu’d-Dawlih, the governor of Yazd
ordered to keep them in his prison for six days, then asked his father who
was the governor of Isfahan what to do next; since no reply arrived then he
fined them and ordered to pay some amount of  money after which they were
released.  After the governor released them, a reply arrived from his father
to keep them in jail and await further instructions. So the governor ordered
his people to arrest these two men again and in addition they arrested five
more Bahá’ís, a total of seven, and put them in jail. With a fatwa issued by
the clergy for their killing because they considered them infidels, the
governor ordered their execution which was carried out on 9 Shawwal 1308
(18 May 1891). Their names and ages in the order of their martyrdom are as
follows:
a) Áqá ‘Alí-Aṣghar Yúzdárání – 19 years of age.
b) Mullá Mihdí Khuvaydakí – 60 years old.
c) Áqá ‘Alí Gázur-Gáhí – 30 years of age.
d) Mullá ‘Alí Sabzivarí – 45 years of age.
e) Áqá Muhammad Báqir – 42 years old
f) Áqá ‘Alí-Aṣghar Fahadani – 24 years old (brother of
Áqá Ḥasan and Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar Káshí)
g) Áqá Ḥasan – 21 years old (brother of Áqá ‘Alí-Aṣghar
#6 above and Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar Káshí)


It should be noted that in the above list of seven martyrs of Yazd, the  story
of martyrdom of whom was given in the text. Three of these martyrs were
related to ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí:
i.
Number 3 in the list: Áqá ‘Alí Gázur-Gáhí who was the
parental cousin of  the father of Mr. ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb
Dhabíḥí’s wife.
ii.
Number 6 in the list: Áqá ‘Alí-Aṣghar Fahadani , the paternal
uncle of Mr. ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí’s wife.
iii.
Number 7 in the list: Áqá Ḥasan who was the parental uncle
of Mr. ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥ’s wife.
For their relationship to ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí refer to Ill. 57, the Family Tree
number 7, page 224.
Haji Muḥammad Ḥusayn Turk’s wife was Zahrá Sulṭán. She had a few
brothers and sisters among whose families were several martyrs. We will list
these individuals below and explain each case:
One of Zahrá Sulṭán’s sisters was Khadijih Sulṭán. She married Zayn’ul-‘Ábidin.
This couple had six children three of whom were martyred, as follows (Sháṭir
Husayn, Sháṭir Ḥasan & Aqa Ali Akbar):
iv.
Sháṭir Ḥusayn (Son of Khadijih Sulṭán and Zayn’ul-‘Ábidin):
On the first day of troubles he went to the house of Ṣadr’ul-
Ulama and was hiding for two days. On 2 Rabí‘u’th-Thání
1321 (28 June 1930) he left the house of Ṣadr and in the very
early hours of morning headed towards his own house located
in the neighbourhood of Kushkak-Naw in Yazd. Just in front
of his house before entering his house he was seen by a
neighbour who reported it to thugs, they came and captured
him, took him to a clergy man, got a fatwas for his killing,
then took him to the government  house and demanded the
governor to kill him by placing him in front of a canon. The
governor was forced to comply with their demand. The victim
was placed in front of a canon which was fired and shredded
him to pieces. He was 25  years  old. A tablet of visitation
was revealed in his honor which is printed on page 340 TAY.
([TAY], pp. 338 – 341).
v.
Sháṭir Ḥasan [son of Zaynu’l-`Abidín and Khadíjih Sulṭán. He
should not be confused with Sháṭir Ḥasan, son of Ibráhim and
a  brother of Muḥammad Ismá‘íl, and also Sháṭir Na’ib Ḥasan].
He was a Bahá’í from the Kushak-Naw quarter of Yazd who,
during this period of unrest in his native town, had moved to
the nearby village of Tazarján, where he was continuing his


profession as a baker. When the people of the village learned
of the disturbances and turmoil in Yazd, they congregated and
surrounded Sháṭir Ḥasan, beating him with stone and sticks,
intending on killing him. With great difficulty and struggle,
Sháṭir Ḥasan was able to escape the crazed mob and quickly
left the village for Manshád. He arrived there on the same day,
three hours before sunset, and took refuge in the home of the
martyred Áqá `Alí-Akbar (son of Aqa Ibáhim) On the first day
of troubles in Manshád, [26 June,] some three hours before
sunset, the esteemed Sháṭir Ḥasan and another believer left
Manshád, out of the fear of the foes, for the village of Darrih.
They arrived there one hour before the sunset and took a room
in Khadíjih Darvísh’s house. When the villagers became aware
of the two newcomers that very night, they surrounded the
house, and a few went inside and captured the two believers.
hands of the two distinguished men were tied behind their back
and they were forced to walk to the nearby village of Darrih;
on the way, they sustain injuries from rocks, stones and sticks
The being thrown at them, they were stoned so severely and
repeatedly that before reaching their destination, they gave life
to the Life-Giver. Sháṭir Ḥasan was thirty-five years old at the
time of his martyrdom on 30 Rabí‘’u’l-Avval  1321 A.H.     (26
June 1903).
vi.
Sháṭir ‘Alí-Akbar, the son of Zaynu’l-`Abidín and Khadíjih
Sulṭán (He should not be confused with ‘Alí-Akbar son of
Áqá Ibráhim.) He and his father Zaynu’l-`Abidín lived in the
neighbourhood of Yazd and engaged in running a bakery.
One day thugs came and took him to Navvab Radavi where
he ordered him to curse the sacred figures of the Faith which
he refused. Thus, Navvab ordered caning his feet very hard,
pulling out his mustache, and after that threw him in a well.
He was almost dead. His relatives arrived there and took him
home and looked after him until he recovered. Then he moved
to a village known as Hedash or Deh-Bala and started bakery
there. He survived the atrocities of 1903. But some years
later, a hooligan approached him and started using bad
language, beating him and kicked him so hard at the side of
his body as a result  of which he offered up his life a few days
later. He was 60 year old at the time of his martyrdom
([TAY], pp. 481-483)


vii.
Áqá `Alí-Muḥammad, the son of Ḥájí Muḥammad
ḤusaynTurk:   Again, on the same Wednesday, 5 Rabí‘u’th-
Thání 1321 (July 1, 1903), the mob went to Kuzh
neighborhood, situated on the outskirts of Manshád on the
way to Káv-Afshád. At that time one of the believers, Áqá
`Alí- Muḥammad, son of Hájí Ḥusayn Turk, was hiding from
the bloodthirsty crowd in the home of Ḥusayn, son of Ḥasan
ibn Panáh-`Alí. Four thugs entered the house; they located the
honored Áqá `Alí- Muḥammad, and took him outside and one
of the gunmen aimed his gun at Áqá `Alí- Muḥammad’s head
and fired. He immediately fell to the ground at which time the
rest of the crowd began stoning him. At that moment, while
the severely injured Áqá `Alí- Muḥammad was breathing his
last breaths, one heartless individual opened Áqá `Alí-
Muḥammad’s mouth, filled it with sand and soil, and then
kicked him in chest until he was dead. Áqá `Alí- Muḥammad
was forty-five years old at the time of martyrdom. ([MAM],
p. 25 & [TSY], 466).
viii.
Haji ‘Alí-Muḥammad Baqir: He was the father of ‘Abdu’l-
Vahháb Dhabíḥí. He was martyred in ‘Abbás-Ábád and the
details of his martyrdom was discussed fully in the main text
of the book.
Another sister of Zahrá Sulṭán was Zaynab Sulṭán. In her family there were
several martyrs who are listed below:
ix.
Ghulám-Ḥusayn Dallal, son of Taqí:  He was the son-in-law
of Zaynab Sulṭán and the husband of Fáṭimih Sulṭán. Fáṭimih
Sulṭán was the maternal cousin of ‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíḥí’s
mother (Sakinih Sulṭán). On Saturday, 15 Rabí`u’th-Thání
1321 A.H. [11 July 1903], Áqá Ghulám-Ḥusayn, son of Áqá
Taqí Dallal [broker], a Bahá’í from Yazd who had come to
Manshád to escape the brutal events unfolding there, along
with another Bahá’í were found hiding in one of the caves in
the eastern mountains, behind the Turks’ farm. Two hours
after sunrise the mob surrounded the cave and called for the
two believers to come out and surrender.  Acquiescing, they
emerged, whereupon Áqá Ghulám-Ḥusayn was killed
instantly by a huge volley of gunfire and later on they
separated his head from his body. He was 63 years old at the
time of his martyrdom. ([TSY], pp. 476-478).


x.
Áqá ‘Alí the son of Ghulám-Ḥusayn Dallal and Fátimih:  Áqá
‘Alí’s wife was one of the daughters of  Sháṭir Na’ib Hassan
whose name was Salṭanat khanum. At beginning of the
turmoil, he had suffered severe injuries and Sháṭir Ḥasan
brought a physician to his house to stich the wounds of Áqá
‘Alí. All of a sudden a mob came and wanted to enter the
house to kill Áqá ‘Alí. At the end the mob killed both Sháṭir
Ḥasan and his son-in-law, Áqá ‘Alí. ([TAY], pp. 178-179)
xi.
Sháṭir Ḥasan had a government position as Sháṭir. He had two
sons and three daughters. The older  daughter was Rubab who
was the wife of Áqá Mírzá Javád Ṣabbágh the martyr and the
other one was Salṭanat khanum the wife of Áqá ‘Alí the
martyr. Sháṭir Ḥasan was put to death in Yazd the same they
that they killed Áqá ‘Alí. ([TAY], p.178)
xii.
Áqá Mírzá Javád Ṣabbágh was the son-in-law of Sháṭir Na’ib
Ḥasan. He was hiding in a hay stack barn. They found him and
with gun bullets they killed him. He was martyred in Hanza of
Yazd on 13 July 1903 (17 Rabí‘u’th-Thání 1321) He was 42
years old. He was survived by two children 11 and 9. His wife
was Rubáb; her father, Sháṭir Ḥasan; her husband, Áqá Mírzá
Javád; and her husband’s brother, Asadu’lláh were all
martyred, ([TAY], pp. 443-446)
xiii.
Mírzá Asadu’lláh Ṣabbágh (son of Mullá Muḥammad
Qannád), the brother of Áqá Mírzá Javád Ṣabbágh. He was a
merchant and did not have any children of his own and was
saying all the Bahá’í youths were his children. During
troubles in Yazd, he was hiding in his neighbour’s house who
was not a Bahá’í. The hooligans broke the door of his house
and entered. They ransacked the house, found him at the end,
and with a knife being thrusted into his neck he was martyred
in the neighbourhood of Pusht-i Bágh. On 25 June 1903 (29
Rabí‘’u’l-Avval 1321 A.H.). He was 45 years old.([TAY],
p.147 & pp. 259-260)
xiv.
Mírzá Ismá‘íl son of Mírzá Ibráhim Khabbáz: When troubles
started, he was in his house in Khadijih farm in Manshád. The
unruly mob found him, repeatedly stabbed him as a result of
which he was martyred. He was 55 on the day of his


martyrdom 27 June 1903 (1 Rabí‘u’th-Thání 1321) ([TAY],
p.65 & p.391)
xv.
Sháṭir Ḥasan, the son of Mírzá Ibráhim Khabbáz and the
brother of Mírzá Ismá‘íl the martyr: On the first day of
troubles in Manshád a few of the friends including three sons
of Mírzá Ibráhim Khabbáz who were: Sháṭir Ḥasan, Áqá ‘Alí-
Akbar and Asadu’lláh (not to be confused with Mírzá
Asadu’lláh Ṣabbágh)  fled to the top of the mountains behind
the farm of Turks. At night when Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar and Sháṭir
Ḥasan were climbing the mountains, they were both injured.
Early in the morning, Sháṭir Ḥasan came down to fetch some
water for his brother who was badly injured and could not
move. Trouble makers spotted him, captured him and killed
him with gun shots. He was 60 year old at the time of his
martyrdom on 29 June 1903 (3 Rabí‘u’th-Thání 1321A.H.)
([TAY], pp. 396-97)
xvi.
Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar son of Mírzá Ibráhim Khabbáz and the
brother of Mírzá Ismá‘íl: He was martyred along with his
brother on 29 June 1903 on top of the mountain where he was
injured and lying down. He was 55 years old. ([TAY], p.398)
xvii.
Mírzá Asadu’lláh son of Mírzá Ibráhim Khabbáz and the
brother of Mírzá Ismá‘íl and Sháṭir Ḥasan (He should not be
confused with Mírzá Asadu’lláh Ṣabbágh)  : On 2 and 3 June
he was with his other two brothers (Áqá ‘Alí-Akbar and
Sháṭir Ḥasan) who were martyred but he came down from the
mountain top and went to town and was hiding in his cousin’s
house. He stayed there for two weeks until things were quiet.
He and another friend left Manshád but on the way at night
he  slept by the road side and in the morning as they started
their walk, they were found and taken to the nearby village of
Ibráhim- Ábád. In that village some hooligans gathered
around him and at the end he was killed with a volley of
bullets on 8 July 1903 (12 Rabí‘u’th-Thání) . He was 35 years
old when he was martyred.
xviii.
Áqá Siyyid Báqir was son-in-law of Ḥájí Zaynal (the brother
of Ḥájí Muḥammad Ḥusayn Truk): Áqá Siyyid Báqir, the son
of Siyyid Aḥmad of Manshád thought the mobs would not
harm him. When they came near his house he invited them in
and offered them food and drink. They went away but came


back and took him to the house of the police chief of the town.
He was imprisoned there. The thugs came and insisted that
they would not kill him in Manshád and they took him outside
of Manshád in Kav-Afshád and killed him with a volley of
bullets. This was on July 11 1903 and 15 Rabí‘u’th-Thání
1321 A.H. He was 52 years old. He was married to Fáṭimih
Sulṭán, the daughter of Ḥájí Zaynal . ([TAY], p. 399)
xix.
Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn son of Siyyid Aḥmad, and the brother of Áqá
Siyyid Baqir: On 4 Rabí`u’th-Thání 1301A.H. (30 June 1903), Áqá
Siyyid Ḥusayn came down from the mountain top where he was
hiding and took refuge in the house of Siyyid Áqá’í. On Friday, 14
Rabí`u’th-Thání 1321 A.H. [10 July 1903], which was now the
fourteenth day of the troubles, the vicious mob was searching every
household in Manshád in hope of finding more Bahá’ís to kill.
Siyyid Áqá’í informed Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn’s wife of the day’s
events, indicating to her that soon his house would be searched and
did not want Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn to be killed in his house. When
Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn learned of the conversation from his wife, he
told her that his death was near. He alone left the home that had
been his family’s refuge, bidding farewell to his wife and son.
Taking the advice of his host, he changed his clothes and took
refuge behind the pulpit of the nearby mosque. His host had asked
him to remain there until the mob had finished searching his house.
Then he would be able to return.
Although the anticipated search of the house did not result in any
findings, a number of women in the neighborhood reported
having seen Siyyid Ḥusayn in the mosque. Siyyid Ḥusayn,
aware of his dire situation, had no choice but to leave the
mosque, running a distance of two hundred yards and then
climbing over a wall into a wheat field. He hid in the wheat but
was spotted by a woman who told the mob. They entered the
field  and searched until they found Siyyid Ḥusayn. Upon
finding him, the mob started striking him with wooden clubs,
then several bullets were fired towards him and the honored
Siyyid Husayn fell down after the very first bullet struck him.
Still some life was left on him when his family came and threw
themselves on his body, and he embraced them tenderly. While
holding his loved ones and several teardrops streaming from his


eyes, Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn’s hallowed spirit left for the Exalted
Paradise. At the time of his martyrdom his age was 40.([MAM],
pp. 31-36 also [TAY], pp. 399-401)

xx.
Áqá Siyyid Javád, the son of Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn (He should not
be confused with Áqá Siyyid Javád the son of Áqá Siyyid Taqi)
:  On 30th June, Áqá Siyyid Ḥusayn, son of Áqá Siyyid Aḥmad
(brother of Áqá Siyyid Baqir), was yet another Bahá’í who had
taken refuge at the home of a Muslim friend, Siyyid Áqá’í. Two
days before Manshád’s convulsions, his eldest son, Siyyid
Javád, who was only fourteen years old, had fallen while
working in the farm fields with his grandfather, breaking one leg
and severely injuring the other. His injuries were so severe that
he could not move and was bedridden. This continued until 10
July when his father was martyred. When he heard the news of
his father’s martyrdom, the bedridden and broken-hearted
Siyyid Javád pleaded for one last opportunity to visit and bid
farewell to his father. Unfortunately, no one paid any attention
to this poor boy, though he was only a short distance away from
his fallen father. After Siyyid Husayn’s death, Siyyid Javád was
taken back to his own home. He was constantly heard saying and
praying, “How I wish that the enemies had seen me and taken
my life on that day too! If only they would come now and cut
my heart out allowing me to join that exalted soul!” Not a day
went by that he did not wish for his own death. His wailing and
lamenting were destined to be as ephemeral as his fleeting life,
for he was to outlive his father’s brutal martyrdom by only
fourteen days. At last Friday, 28 Rabí`u’th-Thání 1321 A.H. (24
July 1903), his spirit soared to the Abhá Kingdom and his
lifeless body in his bed was found by his mother who mourned
for his death. .([MAM], pp. 33-35)

relationships.

114. Refer to Payam Bahá’í, issue number 360-370, August-September 2010, pp.
106-114.
115. Ákhúnd Mullá ‘Abdu’l-Ghaní Ardikání  was one of the outstanding
believers of the early years, a sanctified soul and a respected personage of
Ardikán. He was a learned person and always spent his time in devotion and
contemplation. He was an erudite and an eloquent speaker.  When he was a
youth he was always looking for truth. For sometime he sought the
companies Sufis but at he end his inquisitive mind led him to the Faith. After
he heard the news of the Faith from Ákhúnd Rajab-‘Alí, he abandoned his
role as a Muslim clergy man and devoted all his effort and energy in the
promotion of the new Faith.  After accepting the Faith as the Truth, he
started teaching the Faith which caused an uproar in Ardikán. Hooligans
who were always ready to initiate evil deeds, one night smeared while mud
on the doors of shops, public baths and mosques. The next day, they accused
the Bahá’í that they have done that. Then, they gathered in the house of
Shaykh ‘Alí, the  mujtahid of town, and complained about the ‘Abdu’l-
Ghaní and the Bahá’í. Shaykh ‘Alí ordered to bring ‘Abdu’l-Ghaní to his
presence, so they went and fetched him. On the way they found an
opportunity to severely beat up ‘Abdu’l-Ghaní, using sticks, throwing stone
at him and even strike him with knife. As a result of such vile behaviours of
the mob, before reaching the house of mujtahid, he fainted. In this condition
they took him to the house of Shaykh ‘Alí. Mujtahid said take him away and
the mob intended to take him at the edge of the town, kill him, burn him and
throw him in a deep ditch. On the way they saw a government employee
whom they thought had come to arrest them and all of them ran away. This
man helped ‘Abdu’l-Ghaní reaching home safely. He rested at home and
recovered after sometime. He ignored the troubles and continued teaching
the Faith. Ten years passed in this manner and he became well known as a
Bahá’í teacher among the people of Ardikán. Because of these troubles, his
relatives felt that it was not safe to stay in his own place and changed his
residence to the place of Ṣadr’us-Ṣultán  and after a while he moved to Yazd.
He has received a number of tablets from Bahá’u’lláh. ([TAY]. pp. 120-121
&  Amr-i Bahá’í dar Ardikán, [TAA] pp.  73- 75).
116. Adasiyyih:  Some believes started buying parcels of land in Palestine and
donate them it to the Faith as early as the time of Bahá’u’lláh. Adasiyyih
was a large size piece of farming land located in the north-west of Jordan
which was acquired at the time of the ministry of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. The earliest


settlers were Bahá’ís of Zoroastrian background who moved there from
Yazd. This farming community developed under the guidance and specific
instructions of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá. They introduced innovations in their
agriculture practises which were later on taken up by other farmers in the
area. The size of the community gradually increased. It became a model
development community. ‘Abdu’l-Bahá instructed those Bahá’í farmers to
grow nourishing vegetables and asked them to focus, in particular, on
growing corn. They produced food of high quantities, which they stored in
pits built by the Romans many years earlier on the same land.
When World War I broke out and began to engulf the world. The Ottoman
Empire was embattled, and the war came to Palestine where ‘Abdu’l-Bahá
lived. The community was ravished by famine. ‘Abdu’l-Bahá brought food
from the Bahá’í farming communities in Jordan and fed the starving people
of Haifa, ‘Akká, and the adjacent neighborhoods in the famine years of 1914
to 1918.
Due to unsettled political situation in Palestine, the Guardian decided
to send the believers back to Iran, to their original communities. Many
of them returned to Yazd.

117. A Brief History of Faith in Yazd
Before the time of the Báb
Shaykh Aḥmad Aḥsayyi,  the founder of the Shaykhí school, travelled to
and resided in Yazd for a long period where he wrote the majority of his
works.  In this city, Siyyid Kazim joined him and become a prominent
student and studied under him in Yazd and eventually succeeded him.
During the time of the Báb
After Mullá Husayn’s second visit to shiraz, in the way back to his home-
town, the Báb instructed him to visit Yazd. It was in 1845 also Quddus visited
Yazd briefly.  Around this time Mirza Ahmad Azghandi, a prominent and
one of the most notable believers had moved to Yazd.   Also Mullá Sadiq
Muqaddas Khurani visited the city shortly after the arrival of Mirza Ahmad
Azghandí and delivered a talk at a masjid in which he quoted from the
Writings of the Báb which resulted in him being accused of heresy and he
was almost physically attacked. However, Siyyid Ḥusayn-i-Azghandí
managed to calm the crowd allowing him to leave the place unharmed.
In 1850 Siyyid Yaḥyáy-i-Darábí, entitled Vahíd, visited Yazd and
successfully promoted the Bábí religion, gathering a large number of
followers with people traveling from surrounding villages and nearby towns
to hear him speak. The fledgling community began to experience opposition
and persecution and Vahíd left Yazd and after his departure the house where


he was staying was vandalized. It was during this visit that Haji Husayn Turk
and Raḍir-Ruḥ joined the faith.
In the time of Bahá’u’lláh
In July 1850 the Báb was martyred and in August 1852 two Bábís
independently made an assassination attempt on the life of the Shah of Iran
in retaliation for the execution of the Bab and killing large number of  which
was unsuccessful and this resulted in intense persecution of the Bábí
community and many Bábís in Yazd where martyred,  with many others
leaving the city and immigrating to Baghdad. In 1854 , Ḥájí Mírzá
Muhammad Taqi a cousin of the Báb settled in Yazd and he became a
devoted Bábí after meeting Bahá’u’lláh his visit to Baghdad and meeting
Bahá’u’lláh. He remained in Yazd until 1900. Several Bábí's from Yazd
visited Baghdad during the 1850's and spoke highly of Bahá’u’lláh upon
returning with Mullá Muhammad-Ridá Radiyu'r-Rúh Manshádí delivering a
copy of the Qaṣídiy-i-Varqá'íyyih (Ode of the Dove) to the believers of the
Yazd. Also and Mírzá Husayn, elder brother of Varqá, brought a copy of the
Hidden Words for the believers.
In the 1860's Bahá’u’lláh declared Himself to be Him Whom God shall make
manifest as promised  by the Báb and the majority of the Bábí's of Yazd
accepted His claim becoming Bahá’ís. During the late 1860's Mullá Sádiq
visited Yazd where he successfully convinced several members of the Afnán
family, the family of the Báb, of Bahá’u’lláh’s claims. In 1868 Hájí
Muhammad-Ibrahim and Nabil Akbar visited Yazd and converted Afnan
Kabir (Haji Hasan-.Ali, the uncle of the Bab) who became a prominent
steadfast Bahá’í.
The community faced some persecution and at some point some of the clergy
in Yazd sentenced a Bahá’í, Ḥájí Mullá Mihdí, to death however the mujtahid
of Ardikan refused to confirm the sentence allowing Mihdí to flee to Yazd.
Between 1882 and 1883 Mihdi's son,Varqa , returned to visit Yazd and was
arrested and imprisoned then he was transferred to Isfahan. As of the early
1880's the principal of the Parsi School of Yazd, Ustad Javanmard, was a
Bahá’í, and throughout the 1880's many Zoroastrians of Yazd either became
Bahá’ís or were sympathetic to the religion. In 1888 Edward Granville
Browne visited Yazd and met with members of the Bahá’í community such
as Afnán-i-Kabír. Mirza Abul Fadail and ‘Andalib visited Yazd in 1888 and
met with Shaykh Muhammad, who was promoting the claims of Mírzá
Yaḥyá among the Bahá’í community, and they engaged in a debate which
ended with Shaykh Muhammad insulting them and leaving the meeting and
he departed the city shortly afterwards.


In 1891 a political group based in Yazd began stirring dissent against the
government of Persia and when the government began to take action against
them the group decided to fabricate evidence implying that the Bábí
community was responsible for the dissent. As a result Zillu's-Sultán, the
eldest son of Nasiri-Din Shah, who was involved in the groups activities and
was engaged in an attempt to usurp the throne instigated harsh persecution
of the Bahá’í community of Yazd in order to distract from his involvement
in the situation. On May 18, 1891, seven Bahá’ís were arrested and taken to
the palace of Prince Jalálu'd-Dawla and the Prince asked them what their
beliefs were and reassured them that he wanted to secure their release and
did not intend to persecute them for their beliefs, however when they said
they were Bahá’ís members of the Islamic clergy who were present but
behind a curtain signed their death warrants based on their confession. The
seven men were then publicly executed after refusing to recant their Faith
with their remains being dragged through the streets and then burned. There
was also widespread harassment of Bahá’ís with their properties being
looted, and an estimated one thousand Bahá’ís fled the city to escape and
camped in the open countryside with many of them dying from malnutrition.
The persecution ultimately resulted in increased interest in the Faith in Yazd.
In the time of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá
There were approximately five thousand Bahá’í households in Yazd
as of 1903, and that year ‘Abdu’l-Bahá asked Ibn-Abhar, Hand of the
Cause, to visit the city and during his visit large gatherings were held
which he spoke at to encourage the Bahá’í community, which enthused
the Bahá’í community but also resulted in some opposition from the
Muslim community.
The same year a telegram was forged purporting to be from the Prime
Minister of Persia which prompted Shaykh Taqí, a religious authority in
Isfahan, to issue a fatwa calling for Bahá’ís to be killed. Mírzá Ibráhím, the
Imám-Jum`ih of Yazd, visited Isfahan and upon his return to Yazd on June
12, 1903, he issued an order for the massacre of Bahá’ís in Yazd, and many
Bahá’ís in the city were killed and had their homes destroyed by mobs over
the next two months.
In earl 1900  the Assembly of Yazd formed and started its  operation in 1919
the Bahá’í community began raising funds which were used to assist relatives
of the martyrs of Yazd and construct tombs.
In the time of Shoghi Effendi
In 1921 Muhammad ‘Alí Afnán of Yazd was on pilgrimage in the Holy Land
when ‘Abdu’l-Bahá passed away and he remained in the Holy Land and was


able to associate with the newly appointed Guardian of the Bahá’í Faith,
Shoghi Effendi, until approximately May 1922 when Shoghi Effendi asked
him to return to Yazd where he was elected to the Local Spiritual Assembly
and served for many years often as chairman.(1)
In 1930 Tarazullah Samandari and Martha Root visited Yazd during teaching
tours of Iran. Zikrullah Khadem also visited Yazd in 1930 at the request of
Shoghi Effendi, and in 1933 Keith Ransom Kehler visited Yazd during her
visit to Iran made at the direction of Shoghi Effendi. As of the early 1930's a
large Ḥaẓíratu’l-Quds had been constructed by the Yazd Bahá’í community,
and at some point four Bahá’í owned primary schools were established in the
city however in 1935 the schools were closed down by order of the Persian
Ministry of Education for closing to commemorate Bahá’í Holy Days. As of
1939 the Bahá’í community had constructed a public bathhouse for Yazd
however that year a worker employed to see to the heating of the baths was
injured in a work accident and as a result of prejudice against the Bahá’í
community members of the Local Spiritual Assembly including the chairman
were arrested but were released after only a short period of imprisonment.
In 1947 summer classes for Bahá’í youth were held in Yazd and as of the late
1940's there was a Yazd youth committee which began publishing a local
youth bulletin.(2)
In 1950 a Bahá’í pioneered to a village outside of Yazd and a woman and her
children were murdered shortly afterwards. The murder was attributed to his
arrival and as a result all members of the Local Spiritual Assembly of Yazd
were arrested and sentenced to three years in prison. This caused the Yazd
Bahá’í community to become inactive out of fear of retribution and Shoghi
Effendi asked Dr. Abbas Afnán to pioneer to the city to stimulate the
community in 1952. Afnán remained in the city for two years establishing
deepening classes and holding art exhibitions to stimulate the arts and
members of the community were inspired to become active again.
In the time of the Universal House of Justice
In early 1962 a pioneering conference was held in Yazd,(3) and throughout
1964 forty Bahá’í character training classes for youth and children were held
in Yazd with three-hundred youth and children receiving instruction from
thirty Bahá’ís serving as teachers.(4) As of 1966 the Islamic clergy in Yazd
remained suspicious and belligerent towards the Bahá’í community and the
Bahá’í community was sensitive to provoking hostility from them.
The Revolution in Iran which began in 1979 triggered persecution of the
Bahá’í community and as of July 1980 members of the Yazd Local Spiritual
Assembly had been arrested and five Bahá’ís were injured after being


attacked by a mob in the city. (5) On September 8, 1980, seven prominent
Bahá’ís of Yazd including two Auxiliary Board members were martyred by
revolutionary guards with the executions being justified on local radio with
unsupported accusations of espionage and subversive activities.(6) After the
martyrdoms the bodies were transported to a local hospital and then buried
outside of the city limits the same day in order to prevent large amounts of
Bahá’ís gathering for a funeral. Despite this two-hundred Bahá’ís gathered
for the burial and were able to prepare the bodies for burial according to
Bahá’í law. Two days after the martyrdom a trial of the Bahá’ís was televised
in Yazd however during the broadcast the Bahá’ís defense was rendered
inaudible prompting complaints even from non-Bahá’ís. (7)
In 1981 the assets of 117 Bahá’ís of Yazd were frozen and 150 prominent
Bahá’í families were ordered to report to revolutionary authorities.(8) As of
September 1981 a government official had reportedly privately advised the
Bahá’ís that the persecution in Yazd would potentially be the pattern
followed for persecution of Bahá’ís across the rest of the country. In October
1981 four Bahá’í's imprisoned in Yazd were released however the
community was facing persecution in other forms such as being dismissed
from jobs. As of 1982 over one hundred Bahá’í children had been expelled
from schools in Yazd due to their religion,(9) and Bahá’ís had their
properties looted with houses and furniture being confiscated and most
Bahá’í men were forced to flee the city. As of 1984 some Bahá’ís remained
imprisoned in Yazd and were being tortured by being regularly whipped with
plastic tubes. Some died while imprisoned and their families were sometimes
expelled from their homes after their relatives death. (10)
In 1992 several private homes and other properties belonging to Bahá’ís from
eleven families were confiscated by Islamic revolutionary institutions on the
basis of a religious edict filed by a former Imam of Yazd. A further ten
families had been issued a notice to vacate their homes as they were to be
seized by the government. The Universal House of Justice wrote that the
seizures of Bahá’í properties in Yazd was distinct from seizures in other parts
of the country as it was specifically based on a religious edict and the
authorities in Yazd had ruled that no Bahá’ís could own properties in the city.
As of 1998 houses and properties were still being routinely confiscated from
Bahá’ís in Yazd.(11)
In 2005 another wave of persecution against the Bahá’í community of Yazd
began with several Bahá’ís being arrested for short sentences. After this
Bahá’ís in the city were assaulted, harassed and threatened and the Bahá’í
cemetery of the city being destroyed with bodies being left exposed. In


December 2005 Dhabíhu’lláh Mahramí, a Bahá’í who had been imprisoned
since 1995 on the charge of apostasy, died in a Yazd prison.(12)
In 2011 Bahá’í university students studying in Yazd were expelled from
higher education due to their religion, and in 2014 a Bahá’í in Yazd had a
business license refused due to his religion as it had apparently been ruled
that Bahá’ís should not be granted them.(13)  In 2016 Farhang Amiri was
murdered in Yazd by two men who pretended to be interested in buying a
van from him and after being arrested one of the men stated their motivation
was the fact Amiri was a Bahá’í.(14) When the men were sentenced for the
murder the court noted that according to the Islamic penal code Amiri was
not equal to the accused for the purpose of retributive justice as he was not a
Muslim.
In 2020 some Bahá’ís in Yazd were arrested and sentenced to imprisonment
with the Bahá’í International Community noting this as a sign of persecution
of Bahá’ís in Iran escalating.(15)

118. In this section, the children, grandchildren and great grandchildren of
‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíhí have been listed; and also those of his two
sisters. We start with the listing of  the children and grandchildren of
‘Abdu’l-Vahháb Dhabíhí below:


b. Ḍíyá’u’lláh Dhabíḥí  + Jutta Schliemann;    Children: (1) Tanja,
(2) Cora, (3) Kíyán
